**The prescribed parity (Proved identity, Observed values).** Let F″ = |F|/(2^α3^β) and suppose F″ squarefree, as it is at every member tested (this fails in general, since 23² divides F₃₂ (see Appendix B); without it the identity below holds with the radical R\\\_F in place of F″, and with λ replaced by (−1)^{ω(R\\\_F)}). In the notation F(a, b) = a⁶ − 30a⁴b² − 24a²b⁴ − b⁶ with t = a/b one has 4a\\\_Δ³ + 27b\\\_Δ²c\\\_Δ = −4F(a, b)/d³ for the primitive triple (a\\\_Δ, b\\\_Δ, c\\\_Δ) and d ∈ {1, 2} (checked on all 22 members listed here), so F″ is also the part of |F(a, b)| prime to 6. The sign at a prime p ≥ 5 dividing F is w\\\_p = −(ab/p) for every t, and not only on the members tested: p | F forces p ∤ abc, the integral model Y² = X³ + 4ac³X − 8bc⁵ has v\\\_p(c₄) = 0, so it is minimal with multiplicative reduction, and the reduction is split exactly when −c₆ is a square modulo p, which equals (ab/p) by 27b²c ≡ −4a³ (§4.1). Then ∏\_{p | F″}(−(ab/p)) = (−1)^{ω(F″)}(ab/F″), where (ab/F″) is a Jacobi symbol that can be computed from t without factoring F″, and so W(E\_t) = f·(ab/F″)·λ(F″), with λ the Liouville function. Putting ε = f·(ab/F″), which is computable, W(E\_t) = +1 exactly when λ(F″(t)) = ε, that is when ω(F″) is even if ε = +1 and odd if ε = −1. Along the 16 members 2P, …, 9P + T the values (ω, ε, W) are (2, −1, −1), (1, −1, +1), (2, −1, −1), (3, +1, −1), (2, −1, −1), (2, −1, −1), (3, +1, −1), (2, −1, −1), (4, +1, +1), (3, +1, −1), (4, +1, +1), (5, −1, +1), (4, −1, −1), (4, −1, −1), (5, −1, +1), (4, +1, +1) for 2P, 2P + T, 3P, 3P + T, …, 9P, 9P + T in this order, and W = +1 exactly when λ = ε in all 16 cases; ε takes the value +1 six times and −1 ten times, so the required parity of ω(F″) is not constant: it is even only when ε = +1. A statement of the form "ω is even infinitely often" therefore has to be replaced by "λ(F″(t\_n)) = ε\_n infinitely often". For a member whose F″ is known only up to a composite cofactor C of unknown number of prime factors, the identity shows that W depends on the parity of ω(C) and on (ab/C): at 10P (cofactor of 94 digits, no prime factor below about 25 digits found by elliptic-curve factorization) and 10P + T (78 digits) one gets W = +1 if C is a product of two primes and W = −1 if it is a product of three.

**A correlation form of the problem (Identity; the involution of C₂ Observed).** With ε\_n = f(t\_n)(a\_nb\_n/N\_n) and L\_n = λ(N\_n) for the prime-to-6 part N\_n of |F(a\_n, b\_n)|, F(a, b) = a⁶ − 30a⁴b² − 24a²b⁴ − b⁶, one has W(E\_{t\_n}) = ε\_nL\_n when N\_n is squarefree, so #{n ≤ X : W(E\_{t\_n}) = +1} = X/2 + S(X)/2 with S(X) = Σ\_{n ≤ X} ε\_nλ(N\_n). Positive upper density of positive signs is limsup S(X)/X > −1, and infinitely many positive signs is X + S(X) → ∞; the latter is only a restatement of the claim, not a weaker statement. The Liouville sign depends only on the parity of the valuations: λ(N) = (−1)^{ω(sqf(N))}, whereas the root number involves all primes dividing F, through (−1)^{ω(rad N)}(ab/rad N), so the two agree only for squarefree N; defining L\_n with the radical removes the hypothesis. The two members nP and nP + T of C₂ are related by t ↦ (t + 1)/(t − 1), (a, b) ↦ (a + b, a − b) with a′² + b′² = 2(a² + b²) (the involution M(t) = (10 − 11t)/(10t + 11) with multiplier 221 belongs to C\_{−11/5}, not to C₂). Put G₂(a, b) = F(a + b, a − b) = −54a⁶ + 54a⁴b² + 64a³b³ + 54a²b⁴ − 54b⁶. Then gcd(F, G₂) = 1 and Res(F(t, 1), G₂(t, 1)) = 2¹⁷·7·103·519521², so F(a, b) and G₂(a, b) have no common prime factor outside {2, 7, 103, 519521}, and the numerator of D((t + 1)/(t − 1)) is 27t⁶ − 27t⁴ − 32t³ − 27t² + 27 up to a factor −2, not a multiple of D. On the 3093 pairs (t, (t + 1)/(t − 1)) with t = a/b, |a|, b ≤ 50 (recomputed directly from the definition Π(t) = (−1)^{ω(R\\\_t)}(C\\\_t/R\\\_t) of Appendix D; an earlier figure, mean 0.024 on 4406 pairs, used a separate implementation of f(t) that had not been validated against the table and is superseded), the global factors Π(t) = W(E\_t)/f(t) have joint counts (+, +), (−, −), (−, +), (+, −) equal to 776, 783, 769, 765 and mean product 0.008 (standard error 0.018), so the global factors of the two members of a pair look independent; coprimality of F and G₂ gives disjoint supports of the primes but, as for any such statement, does not by itself give cancellation in Σ λ(N\_n)λ(N\_n′).

**Exact definition of ε (notation).** For t = U/V in lowest terms put F(U, V) = U⁶ − 30U⁴V² − 24U²V⁴ − V⁶ and let F″ = N be the part of |F(U, V)| prime to 6 (the same number as the part prime to 6 of |4a³ + 27b²c| for the triple below, since 4a³ + 27b²c = −4F(U, V)/d³ with d ∈ {1, 2}). In ε(t) = f(t)(ab/F″) the letters a, b denote the **legs of the primitive Pythagorean triple** (a, b, c) = ((V² − U²)/d, 2UV/d, (U² + V²)/d), not the numerator and denominator of t. In t-coordinates, ab = 2UV(V² − U²)/d² and (ab/F″) = (2UV(V² − U²)/F″), because d² is a square. The symbols (U/F″) and (UV/F″), which are what one obtains by reading a, b as the numerator and denominator of t, give the same values as (b/F″) and are **not periodic in n** (no period up to 29 for 2 ≤ n ≤ 60), whereas (ab/F″) has period 6; the modulus itself is not the issue: the part of |F| prime to 6, prime to 2, or the part of |4a³ + 27b²c| prime to 6 give identical symbols on n = 2, …, 60.

**The computable sign ε\_n is periodic in n (Observed for 2 ≤ n ≤ 202; the proof is reduced to two local statements, see the next paragraphs).** An implementation of f(t) = −w₂w₃(2/c) and of ε(t) = f(t)(ab/F″) from the local rules of §4 reproduces the table of this appendix: f agrees in 16 of 16 members and ε = W·λ in 16 of 16. With it, ε\_n and the Jacobi symbol (ab/F″), which needs no factorization, were computed for 2 ≤ n ≤ 202 on both sequences (numerators of up to about 7000 digits). In all 201 values of each sequence,

ε(t(nP)) = +1 if and only if n ≡ 0, 1, 5 (mod 6), and ε(t(nP + T)) = +1 if and only if n ≡ 0 (mod 3),

with no exception. (Since f(t(nP)) = +1, the first statement is a statement about the Jacobi symbol alone; the second combines a Jacobi symbol of period 18 with the finite factor f.) The count of six values ε = +1 among the 16 tabulated members is the count of these classes for n = 2, …, 9. Consequences, when F″ is squarefree: (a) W(E\_{t(nP)}) = ε\_n λ(F″), so the root number is +1 exactly when λ(F″(t\_n)) = +1 for n ≡ 0, ±1 (mod 6) and when λ(F″(t\_n)) = −1 for n ≡ 2, 3, 4 (mod 6), and similarly along nP + T with the classes n ≡ 0 and n ≢ 0 (mod 3); (b) since the product of the two periodic signs is (−1)ⁿ, W(E\_{t\_n})W(E\_{M(t\_n)}) = (−1)ⁿ λ(R\_n), where R\_n is the radical of N(F·G₂) (the two values are coprime by Appendix C), which agrees with the eight tabulated members n = 2, …, 9; (c) Problem H fails only if λ(F″(t\_n)) follows, from some point on, the prescribed periodic pattern of (a) on both sequences. Thus the required parity is not varying irregularly: it is a fixed function of n modulo 6. A proof route: write t\_n = U/V; V is a perfect square (the denominator of x(nP) on an integral model), and Jacobi reciprocity reduces (ab/F″) to characters of the odd parts of U and V² − U², because F ≡ −V⁶, U⁶ or −54V⁶ modulo the primes dividing U, V or V² − U² respectively; periodicity then requires 2-adic and 3-adic control of these numbers along nP, in particular of the 2-adic valuation of U, which has not been carried out.

**Reduction of the periodicity to local statements (Proved reduction; two local checks not yet written out).** (1) *Reciprocity formula (proved).* Let t = U/V in lowest terms with V a perfect square, as for every t\_n (the denominator of x(nP) on an integral model is a square). Without any squarefreeness assumption,

ε(t) = f(t) · ∏\_{v ∈ {2, 3, ∞}} (2t(1 − t²), F(t))\_v · (−1, t)\_v · (−6, 1 − t²)\_v,

where ( , )\_v is the Hilbert symbol and F(t) = f(t) is the sextic. Proof: Hilbert reciprocity for the pair (ab, F) turns (ab/N) = ∏\_{p | N}(ab, F)\_p into the product of the symbols at 2, 3, ∞ and at the primes p ≥ 5 dividing ab; at those primes F ≡ −V⁶, U⁶ or −54V⁶ modulo p according as p divides U, V or V² − U², so the symbol equals (−1/p), 1 or (−6/p) to the power v\_p; a second application of reciprocity to (−1, U) and (−6, V² − U²) removes these primes, and since V is a square the pair (U, V) may be replaced by (t, 1) in every local symbol. The formula agrees with the computed ε in 1014 of 1014 cases (t = U/V, V = D², D ≤ 7, all real positions of t). (2) *Real place (proved).* The product of the three real symbols is +1 for t ≤ 1 and −1 for t > ρ ≈ 5.55, the real root of f; for the integers t = 2, …, 5 it is +1 again. So the sequence t\_n ≤ 1 has no real contribution, and the only exceptions to dependence on residues for integer t in |t| ≤ 6000 are exactly t = 2, 3, 4, 5, as predicted. (3) *Singular points (proved at 0, ±1).* f has no root in ℚ\_2 (none modulo 4) and none in ℚ\_3 (none modulo 3⁵), so F has no zero on the local points. Writing the product as (t, −F)(1 − t, −6F)(1 + t, −6F)(2, F) shows that the residues at t = 0, 1, −1 are −F(0) = 1, −6F(1) = 324 = 18² and −6F(−1) = 324, all squares, so the symbols are locally constant there; near t = ∞ at 2 the symbols reduce to (−1, −6) and (1 − s², −6) with s = 1/t, and 1 − s² ≡ 1 (mod 8) because s ∈ 4ℤ₂ when V is a square. (4) *Local constancy (verified numerically).* Hence ε is locally constant on the points of C₂ over ℚ₂ and ℚ₃, and by compactness ε(t) depends only on t modulo 2^{k₂} and 3^{k₃}. On 3765 points t = U/V with V = D² (D ≤ 16) and (1 − t)(1 + t²) a square in both ℚ₂ and ℚ₃, ε is a function of (t mod 8, t mod 9) with no conflict among 40 classes (and none at the finer levels mod 32, 27); without the condition that t be a local point of C₂ there are conflicts at the 2-adic poles, so that condition is used. (5) *Periodicity.* J₂ has good reduction at 3, so n ↦ nP mod 9 is periodic, and t\_n mod 9 has period 9; the class of t\_n modulo 8 at 2 has period 4 (both checked for 2 ≤ n ≤ 202). Therefore ε\_n is periodic with period dividing 36, and the pattern of period 6 seen on 201 values (more than five full periods) is its exact form. *What remains:* a written verification of the local constancy at the 2-adic points over t = ∞ for the factors f(t) (the rule for w₂ and the symbol (2/c)), and a proof that t\_n mod 8 has period 4 from the structure of J₂(ℚ₂).

**Valuation laws along nP (Observed for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200; consistent with the elliptic divisibility sequence computed to n = 31 in Magma).** Write t\_n = U\_n/V\_n in lowest terms. By the classical identity x(nP) − x(P) = −ψ\_{n−1}ψ\_{n+1}/ψ\_n² (here x(P) = 1), U\_n is, up to a unit and a power of 2, the product W\_{n−1}W\_{n+1} of terms of the elliptic divisibility sequence of P, and V\_n = W\_n². The exact data show, in all 199 cases n = 2, …, 200: v₂(V\_n) = 2v₂(n) for n even and 0 for n odd; v₃(V\_n) = 2v₃(n) if 3 | n and 0 otherwise; v₂(U\_n) = v₂(n² − 1) for n odd and 0 for n even; v₃(U\_n) = v₃(n² − 1) if 3 ∤ n and 0 otherwise (the Magma table of v₂(W), v₃(W), v₂(U), v₃(U) for n ≤ 30 agrees: v\_p(W\_n) = v\_p(n) for p = 2, 3). Two consequences. (a) The 2-adic valuation of U\_n is unbounded along the odd n (it is v₂(n − 1) + v₂(n + 1)), so t\_n approaches 0 in ℚ₂ when n ≡ ±1 modulo a high power of 2; this is exactly the situation covered by the vanishing residue at t = 0 in item (3), so the numerically observed period is not an accident of small n. (b) The class of t\_n modulo 8 at 2 depends only on n modulo 4: for n odd, v₂(U\_n) ≥ 3 gives t\_n ≡ 0 (mod 8); for n ≡ 2 (mod 4), v₂(V\_n) = 2 gives 1/t\_n ≡ 4 (mod 8); for n ≡ 0 (mod 4), v₂(V\_n) ≥ 4 gives 1/t\_n ≡ 0 (mod 8). This settles the second open item above, period 4 of t\_n modulo 8, given the valuation law for p = 2, which is verified here but not proved.

**Splitting ε by place (Observed for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200).** Collect all 2-adic data (the sign −1 of f, the rule w₂, the symbol (2/c) and the Hilbert symbols at 2) into Ψ₂, all 3-adic data (w₃ and the Hilbert symbols at 3) into Ψ₃, and use that the real factor is +1 for t ≤ 1; then ε\_n = Ψ₂(n)Ψ₃(n) (checked against the Jacobi-symbol value of ε\_n for 2 ≤ n ≤ 59, 58 of 58). Along nP, Ψ₃(n) = −1 if 3 | n and +1 otherwise, and Ψ₂(n) = −(−1)ⁿ; along nP + T, Ψ₃(n) is the same and Ψ₂(n) = −1 for every n. So each factor depends only on n modulo 2 or modulo 3, with minimal periods 2 and 3 (and 1 and 3 on nP + T), and the product has periods 6 and 3, as observed. This is the statement that the remaining factors depend only on n modulo 6; its proof is the local analysis of items (3)–(5) above.

**F(U\_n, V\_n) is prime to 6, and the elementary reciprocity identity (Proved from the valuation laws; verified for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200).** Modulo 2, F(U, V) ≡ U⁶ + V⁶, which vanishes only if U and V are both odd; by the valuation laws above V\_n = W\_n² is odd only for n odd, where v₂(U\_n) ≥ 3, so U\_n and V\_n are never both odd and F\_n is odd. Modulo 3, F(U, V) ≡ U⁶ − V⁶, which vanishes only if neither U nor V is divisible by 3; but exactly one of them is (3 | n gives 3 | V\_n, and 3 ∤ n gives 3 | U\_n because v₃(U\_n) = v₃(n² − 1) ≥ 1), so F\_n ≡ ±1 (mod 3). Hence F″\_n = |F\_n| exactly (checked for all 199 values 2 ≤ n ≤ 200; the Magma output for n ≤ 20 shows v₂(F) = v₃(F) = 0 as well). With N = |F\_n|, σ\_F the sign of F\_n, and each X ∈ {U, V − U, V + U} written X = ±2^{i}X′ with X′ odd and positive, quadratic reciprocity gives (X/N) = (±1/N)(2/N)^{i}(σ\_F/X′)(F/X′)(−1)^{((X′−1)/2)((N−1)/2)}, where (F/U′) = (−1/U′) and (F/X′) = (−6/X′) for X = V ∓ U because F ≡ −V⁶ modulo the primes of U and F ≡ −54V⁶ modulo the primes of V ∓ U (and 3 ∤ V ∓ U). Since (V/N) = 1, (ab/F″) = (2/N)(U/N)((V − U)/N)((V + U)/N). This elementary formula equals the directly computed Jacobi symbol in 199 of 199 cases. The single factors are not periodic in n (for example (U/N) is not); only their product is, and that is the statement proved by the Hilbert-symbol analysis above, which groups all the 2-adic and all the 3-adic quantities.

**Closed form of ε\_n along nP and the period-six law (Proved from two facts that are verified for n ≤ 200; the closed form is verified in 199 of 199 cases).** Write m = V\_n² − U\_n². An independent review derived the exact formulas for the symbol J\_n = (U\_n/|F\_n|) (n even: J\_n = (−1/|U\_n|)·sgn(U\_n)^{\[F\_n<0\]}; n odd: J\_n = sgn(U\_n)^{\[F\_n>0\]}) from F\_n ≡ 1 (mod 8) for n even and F\_n ≡ 7 (mod 8) for n odd (both verified, 199 of 199, and the formulas themselves 199 of 199; the congruences follow from the valuation laws since for n odd 8 | U\_n and V\_n is odd, and for n even U\_n is odd and 4 | V\_n). The symbol J\_n is not ε\_n: the correction is K\_n = (2m/|F\_n|), so that ε\_n = J\_nK\_n (199 of 199). Apply reciprocity to K\_n as well: N = |F\_n| ≡ ±1 (mod 8) gives (2/N) = 1; both V − U and V + U are odd (valuation laws), prime to 3, and F ≡ −54V⁶ modulo their primes, which gives (X/N) = (±1/N)(σ\_F/|X|)(−6/|X|)(−1/|X|)^{δ} with δ = 1 if N ≡ 7 (mod 8) and 0 otherwise. Writing (−1/|x|) = sgn(x)χ₄(x) and (−6/|x|) = sgn(x)χ\_{−24}(x) for the odd characters χ₄ and χ\_{−24}, and (6/|x|) = χ₂₄(x) for the even character, all the real signs (those of U, V ∓ U and F) cancel in the product, as the real symbols are +1 for t ≤ 1, and one is left with

ε\_n = χ₄(U\_n)·χ\_{−24}(m) for n even, and ε\_n = χ₂₄(m) for n odd.

Now m ≡ 7 (mod 8) for n even and m ≡ 1 (mod 8) for n odd, and m ≡ 2 (mod 3) if 3 | n, m ≡ 1 (mod 3) otherwise (all three verified, 199 of 199, and consequences of the valuation laws: for n odd m ≡ V² ≡ 1 (mod 8); for n even m ≡ −U² ≡ 7 (mod 8); modulo 3, 3 | n gives 3 | V, m ≡ −U² ≡ 2, and 3 ∤ n gives 3 | U, m ≡ V² ≡ 1). For m ≡ 1 (mod 8) the character χ₂₄(m) equals (m/3), and for m ≡ 7 (mod 8) the character χ\_{−24}(m) equals (m/3). Hence for n odd ε\_n = −1 if 3 | n and +1 otherwise, and for n even ε\_n = χ₄(U\_n)·(m/3) = −(m/3) provided U\_n ≡ 3 (mod 4), i.e. +1 if 3 | n and −1 otherwise. This is exactly ε\_n = +1 if and only if n ≡ 0, 1, 5 (mod 6). The proof therefore rests on two facts that remain to be proved: (i) the valuation laws for the elliptic divisibility sequence at 2 and 3, and (ii) U\_n ≡ 3 (mod 4) for n even (verified for all 100 even n ≤ 200; more precisely U\\\_n ≡ 3 (mod 8) for n ≡ 2 (mod 4) and U\\\_n ≡ 7 (mod 8) for 4 | n, 50 cases each, so the assertion U\\\_n ≡ 7 (mod 8) for all even n is false, as U₂ = 3, and only the congruence modulo 4 is needed; a 2-adic proof would use that for n even the point nP lies in the formal group, where x = w^{−2} − a₂ + O(w²) with a₂ = −2, so that the numerator φ\_n of x(nP) is ≡ 1 (mod 8) and U\_n = V\_n − φ\_n ≡ −1 (mod 4)). The sequence nP + T has not been treated this way.

**Two points of precision for the closed form.** (a) The reduction argument at the additive prime 2 must be stated for the subgroup of points with nonsingular reduction, not for good reduction: modulo 2 the curve is y² = x³ with its cusp at (0, 0), the point P = (1, 1) reduces to a nonsingular point, the group of nonsingular points over 𝔽₂ has order 2, so nP lies in the formal-group subgroup E₁(ℚ₂) exactly when n is even (x(2P) = 1/4 has a pole at 2). The valuation laws v₂(W\_n) = v₂(n) for n even and v₃(W\_n) = v₃(n) for 3 | n (the latter from the good reduction at 3 and v₃(W₃) = 1, W₃ = 3) are verified for n ≤ 200 and are proved in the theorem that follows. (b) The identity W(E\_{t\_n}) = ε\_nλ(F″\_n) holds when F″\_n is squarefree; in general the root number is f(t\_n)(−1)^{ω(R)}(ab/R) with R the radical, which differs from ε\_nλ(F″\_n) when some prime divides F″\_n to a power at least 2. Squarefreeness of F″\_n is false in general (23² divides F₃₂, see the next paragraph), so the period-six law is a statement about ε\_n, and the exact link to W(E\_{t\_n}) is the formula given there.

**Theorem (period six along nP; proved).** Let J₂ : y² = x³ − 2x² + 2x, P = (1, 1), t\_n = 1 − x(nP) = U\_n/V\_n in lowest terms, F\_n = F(U\_n, V\_n), N\_n = |F\_n| and ε\_n = f(t\_n)(ab/F″\_n) with F″\_n the part of N\_n prime to 6. For every n ≥ 2: F″\_n = N\_n, f(t\_n) = +1, and ε\_n = +1 if and only if n ≡ 0, 1, 5 (mod 6). The proof is in the following lemmas; everything is elementary except the standard facts that the denominator of x(nP) on an integral model is a square and that the points of J₂(ℚ₂) with v(x) < 0 together with O form a subgroup E₁ (the kernel of reduction to the nonsingular points).

**Lemma A (the point nP at 2).** (a) For n odd, V\_n is odd and v₂(U\_n) = v₂(n² − 1) ≥ 3. (b) For n even, v₂(V\_n) = 2v₂(n), U\_n is odd, U\_n ≡ 3 (mod 8) if n ≡ 2 (mod 4) and U\_n ≡ 7 (mod 8) if 4 | n; in particular U\_n ≡ 3 (mod 4). *Proof.* Modulo 2 the curve is y² = x³ with its cusp at (0, 0); P = (1, 1) reduces to a nonsingular point, the group of nonsingular points over 𝔽₂ has order 2, so nP ∈ E₁ exactly when n is even. For Q ∈ E₁ put v(x(Q)) = −2k (it is even since y² = x³(1 + …)), k ≥ 1; then v(y) = −3k. *Doubling:* x(2Q) = (x² − 2)²/(4x(x² − 2x + 2)); here v((x² − 2)²) = −8k and v(4x(x² − 2x + 2)) = 2 − 2k − 4k, so v(x(2Q)) = −2k − 2, that is k increases by exactly 1. *Odd multiples:* \[m\]Q for m odd has the same k, by induction on m using the formal group law F(z₁, z₂) = z₁ + z₂ + z₁z₂(…) over ℤ₂ and v(z(Q)) = k (here z = −x/y): \[m\]Q = Q + \[m − 1\]Q with v(z(\[m − 1\]Q)) > k. Now x(2P) = 1/4 has k = 1. For n = 2ˢm with m odd, nP = 2^{s−1} has k = s, so v₂(V\_n) = 2s, and U\_n is odd because gcd(U\_n, V\_n) = 1. To find U\_n mod 8, use the expansion x = z⁻² + Σ\_{j≥0} c\_jz^j with c\_j ∈ ℤ₂ (c₀ = −a₂ = 2): write V\_n = 2^{2s}D′² with D′ odd, z = 2ˢu with u a unit; then φ\_n := x(nP)V\_n = u⁻²D′² + (terms of 2-adic valuation ≥ 3) ≡ 1 (mod 8), because the square of a 2-adic unit is ≡ 1 (mod 8). Since U\_n = V\_n − φ\_n, U\_n ≡ −1 (mod 4); and V\_n ≡ 4 (mod 8) for s = 1, V\_n ≡ 0 (mod 16) for s ≥ 2 gives the residues modulo 8. (a) For n odd nP ∉ E₁ and V\_n is odd. For R ∈ E₁ with v(x\_R) = −2k, the chord formula gives x(P + R) − 1 = (x\_R − 2y\_R + 1)/(x\_R − 1)² (checked on 38 cases). If k ≥ 2, v(x\_R − 2y\_R + 1) = 1 − 3k because 1 − 3k < −2k, and v((x\_R − 1)²) = −4k, so v(x(P + R) − 1) = k + 1. For n ≡ 1 (mod 4) take R = (n − 1)P, k = v₂(n − 1) ≥ 2; for n ≡ 3 (mod 4) use nP = (n + 1)P − P, so x(nP) = x(P + R′) with R′ = −(n + 1)P and k = v₂(n + 1) ≥ 2. Since t\_n = 1 − x(nP) and V\_n is odd, v₂(U\_n) = v₂(t\_n) = k + 1 = v₂(n² − 1) ≥ 3. □

**Lemma B (the point nP at 3).** J₂ has good reduction at 3 (its discriminant is −2⁸) and P has order 3 modulo 3 (2P ≡ (1, 2) = −P over 𝔽₃). Hence 3 | n if and only if 3 | V\_n, and if 3 ∤ n then x(nP) ≡ 1 and 3 | U\_n; exactly one of U\_n, V\_n is divisible by 3. □

**Lemma C.** Modulo 2, F ≡ U⁶ + V⁶, and U, V are never both odd by Lemma A; modulo 3, F ≡ U⁶ − V⁶, nonzero by Lemma B; so F\_n is prime to 6 and F″\_n = N\_n. By Lemma A, F\_n ≡ −V⁶ ≡ −1 ≡ 7 (mod 8) for n odd (as 8 | U\_n) and F\_n ≡ U⁶ ≡ 1 (mod 8) for n even (as 4 | V\_n), so N\_n ≡ ±1 (mod 8) and (2/N\_n) = 1. Also V\_n ± U\_n is odd and prime to 3, V\_n is a square, and t\_n ≤ 1 since x ≥ 0 on the real curve. □

**Lemma D (f = +1).** In the triple, 3 | U\_nV\_n gives 3 | b, so B ≡ 0 (mod 3) and w₃ = +1. For n odd, 8 | U\_n gives (A, B) ≡ (1, 0) (mod 16) and c ≡ 1 (mod 8); for n ≡ 2 (mod 4), (A, B) ≡ (15, 8) (mod 16); for 4 | n, (A, B) ≡ (15, 0) (mod 16), with c ≡ 1 (mod 8) in both even cases. All three pairs are in the list of the rule for w₂, so w₂ = −1, (2/c) = 1, and f = −w₂w₃(2/c) = +1. □

**Lemma E (the closed form).** Write m = V² − U², N = N\_n, σ\_F the sign of F, δ = 1 if N ≡ 7 (mod 8) and 0 otherwise (so δ = \[F > 0\] for n odd and δ = \[F < 0\] for n even), and χ₄(x) = (−1)^{(x−1)/2} for odd x. As (V/N) = 1 and (2/N) = 1, (ab/N) = (U/N)((V − U)/N)((V + U)/N). For X = V ∓ U (odd, prime to 3) reciprocity gives (X/N) = (σ\_X/N)(σ\_F/|X|)(−6/|X|)χ₄(|X|)^δ, since F ≡ −54V⁶ modulo the primes of X; for U = ±2^iU′, (U/N) = (σ\_U/N)(σ\_F/U′)χ₄(U′)^{1+δ}, since F ≡ −V⁶ modulo the primes of U. Also (−1/N) = (−1)^δ. *n odd:* the exponent of χ₄(U′) is \[F < 0\] + 1 + δ = 2, the exponent of χ₄(|m|) is \[F < 0\] + δ = 1, and (−6/|m|)χ₄(|m|) = (6/|m|), so (ab/N) = (−1)^{δ·#{negative among U, V − U, V + U}}(6/|m|); since t\_n ≤ 1 and δ = 1 only for t < −ρ, where two of the three are negative, the sign is +1 and (ab/N) = (6/|m|) = (m/3), using m ≡ 1 (mod 8). *n even:* the exponent of χ₄(U′) is 1 and that of χ₄(|m|) is even, so (ab/N) = (−1)^{δ·#neg}χ₄(|U|)(−6/|m|); with χ₄(|U|) = sgn(U)χ₄(U) and (−6/|m|) = sgn(m)χ₋₂₄(m), a check over the intervals t < −ρ, (−ρ, −1), (−1, 0), (0, 1) shows that all real signs cancel, so (ab/N) = χ₄(U)χ₋₂₄(m) = −(m/3), using U ≡ 3 (mod 4) and m ≡ 7 (mod 8). By Lemmas A and B, m ≡ 1 (mod 8) for n odd, m ≡ 7 (mod 8) for n even, and m ≡ 2 (mod 3) if 3 | n, m ≡ 1 (mod 3) otherwise. So (ab/N) = −1 if n is odd and 3 | n, +1 if n is odd and 3 ∤ n, +1 if n is even and 3 | n, −1 if n is even and 3 ∤ n, which is the stated pattern (+1 for n ≡ 0, 1, 5 and −1 for n ≡ 2, 3, 4 modulo 6). □ The lemmas were checked numerically for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200, and the case distinctions agree with the values computed there. The link to W(E\_{t\_n}) still requires F″\_n squarefree (see above); the sequence nP + T is not covered.

**The prime divisors of F\_n do not form a divisibility sequence (Computed; negative result).** A Magma run (complete factorizations for n ≤ 7, trial division to 10⁴ up to n = 18) agrees with the table of this appendix: for n = 2, …, 7 the number of prime factors is 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4, the Liouville values are +1, +1, +1, −1, +1, +1, no prime is repeated, and F\_n > 0 only for n = 7, 10, 17 among n ≤ 18. It also showed that F\_m ∤ F\_n for all m | n tested and that gcd(F\_m, F\_n) = 1 whenever gcd(m, n) = 1 for 2 ≤ m < n ≤ 18. The second pattern does not persist. For all pairs 2 ≤ m < n ≤ 70 (exact integers, prime-to-6 parts) 132 pairs have a common prime, and 28 of them have gcd(m, n) = 1: for example 307 divides F₃, F₂₃, F₃₇ and F₅₃, 2851 divides F₅ and F₄₈, 631 divides F₆ and F₆₇, 547 divides F₁₀ and F₄₁. The explanation is that p | F\_n exactly when nP ≡ ±R\_i (mod p) for the points R\_i of J₂(𝔽\_p) over the roots of f, so the set of such n is a union of up to six classes ±a\_i modulo the order m\_p of P, with no relation between different indices: for p = 23, m\_p = 10 and n ≡ ±2 (mod 10), so only even indices share 23, which produced the apparent pattern; for p = 83, m\_p = 39 and n ≡ ±12, ±16; for p = 307, m\_p = 163 and n ≡ ±3, ±23, ±37, ±53. Hence F\_n is not a divisibility sequence and the Liouville values λ(F″\_n) at different n have no multiplicative or divisibility structure to exploit.

**F″\_n is not squarefree in general (Proved by exact modular computation).** Computing ψ\_k(P) modulo p³ by the division-polynomial recursion (checked against the exact values for p ∈ {23, 83, 307} and 2 ≤ n ≤ 40, 117 of 117) gives v₂₃(F₃₂) = 2, v₈₃(F₆₁₂) = 2 and v₃₀₇(F₃₈₅₉) = 2 exactly, so 23² | F₃₂, 83² | F₆₁₂ and 307² | F₃₈₅₉. A search over all n ≤ 400 and primes p < 6000 finds p² | F\_n only at (n, p) = (32, 23), (198, 23) and (262, 23); primes above 6000 were not searched. The root number is nevertheless given by the exact formula W(E\_{t\_n}) = f(t\_n)(−1)^{ω(R)}(ab/R), R the radical of F″\_n, since a prime p ≥ 5 dividing F gives multiplicative reduction of type I\_k with local sign w\_p = −(ab/p) whatever k is. Comparing with ε\_nλ(F″\_n) = f(ab/F″)(−1)^{Ω(F″)} one finds W(E\_{t\_n}) = ε\_nλ(F″\_n)·∏\_{p : v\_p(F″\_n) even} w\_p, so the identity W = ε\_nλ(F″\_n) holds exactly when every prime divides F″\_n to an odd power. At the three examples w₂₃ = +1 (n = 32), w₈₃ = −1 (n = 612) and w₃₀₇ = +1 (n = 3859); F₃₂ has not been factored, so ω(R) is not known there. The period-six law for ε\_n is unaffected, since its proof does not use squarefreeness; what changes is that the statement "W = +1 if and only if λ(F″\_n) = ε\_n" must be replaced, at indices where some prime has an even exponent, by "W = +1 if and only if f(−1)^{ω(R)}(ab/R) = +1", and Problem H, which asks for infinitely many n with this sign +1, is unchanged in nature.

**Test cases for the non-squarefree branch of the root-number formula (to be checked independently).** Among the 190 values t = u/v with |u|, v ≤ 60 for which F(u, v) is divisible by the square of a prime p ≥ 5, in 94 the exact formula W = f(t)(−1)^{ω(R)}(ab/R) and the naive expression ε·λ(F″) give different signs, so an independent root-number computation decides between them. The simplest is t = −4, where (a, b, c) = (−15, −8, 17), F = 4a³ + 27b²c = 15876 = 2²·3⁴·7², and the integral model is Y² = X³ − 294780X + 90870848; the naive expression gives −1 and the exact formula +1. Others (prime with even exponent in brackets, naive, exact): t = 5/11 (7², naive +1, exact −1), −3/13 (7², +1, −1), 9/10 (7², −1, +1), 7/13 (19², +1, −1), −5/22 (23², +1, −1), 17/8 (7², −1, +1). A control where both agree: t = 4 (7², −1, −1). Since the earlier checks of the formula against Magma used the radical form of the theorem, these curves test exactly the case in which the squarefree shortcut fails.

**Proposition (infinitely many non-squarefree values; proved).** For every integer k ≥ 0, 23² | F\_{32+230k}, 83² | F\_{612+3237k} and 307² | F\_{3859+50041k}. In particular F″\_n = |F\_n| is not squarefree for infinitely many n. *Proof.* J₂ has good reduction at every odd prime (discriminant −2⁸). Let p be odd and r\_p the order of P modulo p (10, 39, 163 for p = 23, 83, 307). The kernel of J₂(ℤ/p²ℤ) → J₂(𝔽\_p) has exponent p, so p·r\_p·P ≡ O (mod p²) and the class of nP modulo p², hence the class of t\_n modulo p², is periodic in n with period dividing p·r\_p. For p | F\_n the denominator V\_n is a unit modulo p and F\_n = V\_n⁶f(t\_n), so the condition p² | F\_n depends only on n modulo p·r\_p. The values n = 32, 612, 3859 were computed exactly modulo p³ above (v\_p = 2). □

**The mechanism: one lift per class (proved when r\_pP ≢ O mod p²; verified for 55 primes).** If p | F\_{n₀} then t\_{n₀} reduces to a simple root r of f modulo p (f is separable modulo p, and r is not a branch value 1, ±i of the degree-2 map t: J₂ → ℙ¹, because f(1) = −54 and f(±i) = −8). For n = n₀ + j·r\_p one has nP = n₀P + jD with D = r\_pP in the kernel of reduction, z(jD) = jz(D) + O(p²) in the formal group, so t(nP) ≡ t\_{n₀} + j·p·u (mod p²) with u a unit when D ≢ O (mod p²); thus f(t\_n) ≡ p(a + j f′(r)u) (mod p²) has exactly one solution j modulo p. Hence modulo p·r\_p there is exactly one n with p² | F\_n for each class of n modulo r\_p with p | F\_n (classes come in pairs ±a, since t\_{−n} = t\_n). The computation confirms this for the three primes (2, 4, 8 classes and 2, 4, 8 lifts modulo 230, 3237, 50041) and for all 55 primes p < 3200 that divide some F\_n (23, 83, 149, 197, 307, 467, 541, 547, …): in every case the number of lifts equals the number of classes, so r\_pP ≢ O (mod p²) always occurred. Summing (number of classes)/(p·r\_p) over these primes gives a density of about 0.0107 of indices n for which F\_n is divisible by the square of one of them (0.0087 from p = 23 alone), which predicts about 4 such n ≤ 400 against the 3 found. Squarefree values are therefore the majority, but not all, and the local root-number factor of the primes with even exponent must be kept in the exact formula for W above.

**Squarefree kernel and the even-valuation correction (Proved identities; densities heuristic).** Write |F\_n| = d\_ns\_n² with d\_n the product of the primes p with v\_p(F\_n) odd, χ\_p(n) = (g(t\_n)/p) with g(t) = 2t(1 − t²), and A\_n = (−1)^{ω(d\_n)} = λ(F″\_n) (the Liouville value depends only on the primes of odd valuation). Since W(E\_{t\_n}) = f(t\_n)∏\_{p | F\_n, p ≥ 5}(−χ\_p(n)) for every valuation, one has exactly W = ε\_n·A\_n·𝒞\_n with 𝒞\_n = ∏\_{p | F\_n, v\_p even}(−χ\_p(n)) and ε\_n = ∏\_{p | d\_n}χ\_p(n) = (ab/d\_n), the periodic sign of the Theorem above; so A\_n is the Liouville core of Problem H, not a new quantity. Restrictions on d\_n: since s\_n is prime to 6, s\_n² ≡ 1 (mod 24) and σ\_Fd\_n ≡ F\_n (mod 24), and F\_n mod 24 depends only on n mod 6 (checked for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200: 1 if n ≡ 0, 17 if n ≡ 2, 4, 7 if n ≡ 3, 23 if n ≡ 1, 5), together with the Jacobi condition (ab/d\_n) = ε\_n. The correction 𝒞\_n is +1 whenever F\_n is squarefree. For a prime p the sign χ\_p(n) depends only on the class of n modulo r\_p, hence is constant for the primes with a single pair of classes ±a (p = 23: χ = −1 always, local factor +1; p = 83, 149, 197: χ = +1, local factor −1); only primes with four or more classes (307, 631, 823, …) have both values. Summing over the 55 primes p < 3200 that divide some F\_n, the density of indices with p² | F\_n and local factor −1 is about 0.0017 and with local factor +1 about 0.0090 (mostly from p = 23). So 𝒞\_n = +1 for about 99% of indices, but this is a heuristic statement: the density of n with F\_n squareful is at least 0.0087 (proved, from p = 23 alone), whether 𝒞\_n = −1 occurs infinitely often cannot be proved without control of the other primes of even valuation, and in any case a change of the target sign on a set of density ≈ 0.2% does not touch the Liouville obstruction in A\_n.

**Consecutive values: no square-class mechanism (Proved in the function field; supported at the points).** Let h(Q) = f(1 − x(Q)) on J₂ and Q ↦ Q + P the translation. With x(Q ± P) = (x² − x + 2 ∓ 2y)/(x − 1)² one finds t(Q ± P) = (±2y − x − 1)/(x − 1)², and the product N₊N₋ of the numerators of f(t(Q + P)) and f(t(Q − P)) is a polynomial in x, equal to (x − 1)¹²H(x) with H of degree 12, irreducible over ℚ and coprime to F(x) = f(1 − x). Since the conjugation of ℚ(J₂)/ℚ(x) sends Q to −Q, N(h(Q)h(Q + P)) = h(Q)²·h(Q + P)h(Q − P) = F(x)²H(x)/(x − 1)¹², which is not a square in ℚ(x). For any r ∈ ℚ(x)^× the norm of r·h(Q)h(Q + P) is r²·N(h(Q)h(Q + P)), again a nonsquare, so h(Q)h(Q + P) ∉ ℚ(x)^×·ℚ(J₂)^{×2} (constants included). This excludes the simplest function-field mechanism for the signs at consecutive indices; it does not exclude identities with functions involving y, and the relevant identity for the torsion translation (Appendix D) was not a function-field identity but a point-specific one at the roots of f. The point-specific test for the translation by P gives the same negative answer: for the 4151 points R of J₂(𝔽\_p) over the roots of f, p < 40000, the character (g(t(R + P))/p) is not determined by p modulo 24 and by χ(R) = (g(t(R))/p) (the best predictor is right for 52.5% of the points, 50% being no information), the same holds for (c(t(R + P))/p), and the product of the two characters takes the values +1 and −1 equally often (2056 and 2095). So among the translations tested only that by the 2-torsion point T produces a constant square-class identity at the roots of f; the doubling (negative norm, Appendix D) and the translation by P do not. A square-class identity would in any case constrain quadratic characters, not the Liouville values λ(F″\_n).

**x(nP) is a rational square for every n (Proved; explains the 2-isogeny experiment).** The point P′ = (2, 4) lies on J₂′ : Y² = X³ + 4X² − 4X and the dual isogeny J₂′ → J₂ has x(φ̂(Q)) = (Y/(2X))², so x(φ̂(P′)) = 16/16 = 1 = x(P); hence P = ±φ̂(P′) (this is the statement that the class x(P) = 1 of P in ℚ^×/ℚ^{×2} is trivial) and nP = φ̂(nP′), so x(nP) = (Y\_n/(2X\_n))² is the square of a rational number for every n (checked for 1 ≤ n ≤ 200, 200 of 200, while x(nP + T) is a square in none of the 40 cases tested). Consequences. (a) With x(nP) = m\_n²/D\_n², V\_n = D\_n² and V\_n − U\_n = m\_n², so t\_n = (D\_n − m\_n)(D\_n + m\_n)/D\_n² and U\_n = (D\_n − m\_n)(D\_n + m\_n) (58 of 58 cases). (b) For an odd prime p the image φ̂(J₂′(𝔽\_p)) has index 2 in J₂(𝔽\_p), because ker φ̂ has order 2 and #J₂ = #J₂′, and it contains P; therefore the order of P modulo p divides #J₂(𝔽\_p)/2 and the index \[J₂(𝔽\_p) : ⟨P⟩\] is even for every odd prime p. This is the pattern seen for all 668 odd primes below 5000 in the experiment (even index 668, odd index 0, P in the dual-isogeny image 668 of 668) and is not a separate phenomenon; in particular P is a primitive root for no prime. (c) A prime p can divide F\_n only at a root r of f modulo p for which 1 − r is a square modulo p (and, as before, (1 − r)(1 + r²) is a square): this holds for all 158 classes found among the primes p < 3200, while only 212 of the 450 roots of f modulo these primes satisfy it, so it cuts the candidate pairs (p, r) roughly in half. (d) F\_n = F(ξ\_n²) with ξ\_n = m\_n/D\_n, but f(1 − ξ²) is irreducible of degree 12 over ℚ, so F\_n does not factor into two rational values A(ξ\_n)A(−ξ\_n). None of this controls the Liouville values λ(F″\_n).

**Coprimality of the two sextic values along C₂ (Proved by a finite computation).** Let J₂ : y² = z³ − 2z² + 2z, P = (1, 1), t\_n = 1 − z(nP) = a\_n/b\_n in lowest terms, F(a, b) = a⁶ − 30a⁴b² − 24a²b⁴ − b⁶ and G₂(a, b) = F(a + b, a − b), and let N(m) be the part of |m| prime to 6. Then gcd(N(F(a\_n, b\_n)), N(G₂(a\_n, b\_n))) = 1 for every n ≥ 1 for which t\_n is finite. Proof: gcd(a\_n + b\_n, a\_n − b\_n) divides 2, so for odd p a common prime factor p must divide Res(F(t, 1), G₂(t, 1)) = 2¹⁷·7·103·519521² and t\_n mod p must be a common root of F and G₂ in 𝔽\_p (the point at infinity is not a root of F). The common roots are t = 4 for p = 7, t = 66 for p = 103 and t = 134939, 482030 for p = 519521. J₂ has good reduction at odd primes (its discriminant is −2⁸), the reduction of P has order 6, 54 and 698 modulo 7, 103 and 519521, and listing z(nP) over a full period shows that t(nP) = 1 − z(nP) is never a common root (for p = 7 and p = 103 the common root is moreover not the t-coordinate of any 𝔽\_p-point of the cover w² = (1 − t)(1 + t²), the value of the right-hand side being a non-residue; for p = 519521 both roots do have points above them, and the exclusion comes from the orbit). The statement was also checked directly on the members n = 2, …, 11. By contrast the analogous statement for the reflection pairs of C\_{−11/5} is false (the prime 3532913 divides both values at n = 20062, see above). Since the Liouville function is completely multiplicative, coprimality is not needed for λ(A\_nB\_n) = λ(A\_n)λ(B\_n); it matters only for the independence heuristics, because a prime dividing both values would contribute a local factor to both signs.

At all four members the point (2, y) is non-torsion (no multiple up to 12 is the point at infinity). For 8P, F(t) = 2²·23·541·p₂₆·p₄₃ with a 26-digit and a 43-digit prime: my own elliptic-curve factorization (B₁ up to 250000, 156 curves) missed the 26-digit factor, and Magma's Factorization found it in a few seconds. I verified that 4·23·541·p₂₆·p₄₃ = |F| and that all four factors are probable primes, and recomputed the Legendre symbols independently. The members 9P and 9P + T (F of 91 digits after removing 2 and 3) were also factored completely, with an elliptic-curve program of my own in a few minutes each (the 63-digit prime factor at 9P + T and the 42-digit one at 9P are probable primes), and give W = +1 at both; the next members 10P and 10P + T have F of 113 digits.

**Why it is hard.** (i) s(t) is the parity of the number of prime factors (with a character twist) of the values of an irreducible sextic form, so H is a Liouville-type statement; the unconditional results of Helfgott and Desjardins require all irreducible factors of the polynomial of multiplicative reduction to have degree ≤ 3, and here the degree is 6 and remains divisible by 6 after any rational base change (§6). (ii) The values are taken along the thin sequence nP, with heights growing like e^{n²}. (iii) Making F equal to a constant times a square infinitely often is impossible (Faltings, genus 2).

**Structure available to a solver.** A prime p ≥ 5 divides F(t(R)) exactly when R reduces modulo p to one of the 12 points of E′ lying over the six roots of D(t) = t⁶ − 30t⁴ − 24t² − 1 (two points over each root, E′ → ℙ¹\_t having degree 2). Hence for fixed p the condition on n is a congruence modulo the order of P in E′(𝔽\_p) (a rank-of-apparition condition, as for elliptic divisibility sequences), and the primes dividing F(t\_n) are the primitive divisors of a family of such sequences. A proof of H could use this, or could show the equivalent statement for the parity of the number of such primes along n in an arithmetic progression.

**Exact local periods (3-adic part Proved, 2-adic part Observed).** Along t\_n the 2-adic factor is w₂ = −1 and the 3-adic factor w₃ = +1 for all n ≤ 534, so f(t\_n) = +1. Along t′\_n the factor w₂ alternates +, −, +, − (period 2) and w₃ has period 9 with the pattern + − + + + + − + + for n = 1, …, 9, hence f has period 18. For p = 3 this is proved: E′ has good reduction at 3 with |E′(𝔽₃)| = 6, w₃ depends on (A, B) mod 9, hence on the point modulo 9, hence on the image of the point in the finite group E′(ℤ/9) of order 18; so the orbit n ↦ nP + jT is periodic with period dividing 18, and the values for n ≤ 18 were computed exactly. For p = 2 the periods 1 and 2 are established by computation up to n = 534; a proof needs the analogous finite quotient of E′(ℚ₂), which I did not verify. Along nP, Problem H is therefore exactly the statement that s(t\_n) is even for infinitely many n, and along nP + T the statement that (−1)^{s(t′\_n)} = f(n mod 18) infinitely often.

**Arithmetic form of s(t).** Since ab ≡ 2t(1 − t²) modulo squares, a prime p ≥ 5 dividing F(t) with t ≡ r (mod 𝔭) for a root r of D in 𝔽\_p is of split type exactly when 2r(1 − r²) is a square modulo p. Thus s(t) counts the primes of degree one of the sextic field ℚ(r) (r a root of D) that divide t − r and split in ℚ(r)(√(2r(1 − r²))); H is a statement about the parity of this count along t = t\_n.

**The twelve points (Observed).** Let Q\_j ∈ E′(ℚ̄) be the twelve points over the six roots of D; the two points over a root are ±Q\_i. The polynomial R(w) = w¹² − 66w¹⁰ − 29922w⁸ + 73214w⁶ − 62559w⁴ + 22032w² − 3456, whose roots are their w-coordinates, is irreducible, so they form one Galois orbit (the norm of (1 − r)(1 + r²) is −54·64 = −3456, not a square). For the 3241 primes 7 ≤ p < 30000 other than 23, the number of roots of R modulo p follows the distribution of fixed points of the group C₂⁶ ⋊ S₄ of order 1536 acting on the 12 points: the frequencies of 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12 roots are 0.687, 0.189, 0.096, 0.013, 0.0105, 0.0034, 0.0003 observed against 0.678, 0.191, 0.104, 0.013, 0.0098, 0.0039, 0.0007 predicted. So the Galois group appears to be the full wreath product, the largest group compatible with the block structure ±Q\_i. The function D(t) has divisor Σ\_j Q\_j − 12·O on E′, so the relation Σ\_j Q\_j = O is the only one it forces, and it is trivial because Q\_j and −Q\_j occur together. A prime p ≥ 5 divides F(t\_n) exactly when nP ≡ ±Q\_i modulo a prime above p for some i, a rank-of-apparition condition.

**Consequence.** If H holds, there are infinitely many t with rank E\_t(ℚ) ≥ 2 or Sha(E\_t) infinite (§6), hence rank ≥ 2 if Sha(E\_t)\[2^∞\] is finite; similar statements hold for the other covers C\_c of §6.

**A test-bed.** The following Python script (with `sympy`) computes t\_n, the primes ≥ 5 of F(t\_n) with their split type, the finite part f and the sign W for n ≤ 6. For larger n replace `factorint` by an external factorization of F (for example PARI or ECM).

```python
from fractions import Fraction as Fr
from math import gcd
from sympy import factorint, jacobi_symbol

a2, a4 = -2, 2   # E': v^2 = z^3 - 2 z^2 + 2 z

def add(P, Q):
    if P is None: return Q
    if Q is None: return P
    x1, y1 = P; x2, y2 = Q
    if x1 == x2:
        if y1 == -y2: return None
        lam = (3*x1*x1 + 2*a2*x1 + a4) / (2*y1)
    else:
        lam = (y2 - y1) / (x2 - x1)
    x3 = lam*lam - a2 - x1 - x2
    return (x3, lam*(x1 - x3) - y1)

def trip(u, v):
    d = gcd(gcd(abs(v*v - u*u), abs(2*u*v)), v*v + u*u)
    return (v*v - u*u)//d, (2*u*v)//d, (v*v + u*u)//d

def w2(A, B):
    if A % 2 == 1:
        if A % 4 == 3:
            return 1 if B % 8 == 4 else -1
        s = (-3*A + 27*B) % 16
        if B % 8 == 4:
            return 1 if s in (9, 13) else -1
        return 1 if s in (5, 9) else -1
    return 1 if B % 4 == 3 else -1

def w3(A, B):
    if B % 3 == 0 or A % 9 in (0, 6): return 1
    return 1 if (4*B) % 9 in (4, 7, 8) else -1

P, T = (Fr(1), Fr(1)), (Fr(0), Fr(0))
R = None
for n in range(1, 7):
    R = add(R, P)
    for lab, Q in ((str(n) + "P", R), (str(n) + "P+T", add(R, T))):
        t = 1 - Q[0]
        a, b, c = trip(t.numerator, t.denominator)
        Fv = 4*a**3 + 27*b*b*c
        if Fv == 0: continue
        s = sum(1 for p in factorint(abs(Fv)) if p >= 5
                and jacobi_symbol((a*b) % p, p) == 1)
        A16, B16 = a*pow(c, -1, 16) % 16, b*pow(c, -1, 16) % 16
        A9, B9 = a*pow(c, -1, 9) % 9, b*pow(c, -1, 9) % 9
        f = -w2(A16, B16)*w3(A9, B9)
        print(lab, t, "s =", s, "f =", f, "W =", f*(-1)**s)
```

**The same problem on the cover C₄′.** Let C₄′ : v² = w³ + 8, with R = (1, 3) of infinite order and the 2-torsion point T = (−2, 0), and let t(R′) = (w(R′) − 2)/(w(R′) + 2); the members are t(nR) = −1/3, −15, 191/675, 25665/36481, −17822401/8782563, … and t(nR + T) = 0, 11/12, −104/121, −28369/8112, 3723720/6651241, …. (Translation by T acts on the t-line by t ↦ −(1 + 3t)/(3(1 − t)).) Here c is not a perfect square, so the factor (2/c) enters the finite part f(t) = −w₂(t)w₃(t)(2/c). Along nR, f(t(nR)) = (−1)ⁿ (period 2), and along nR + T, f = +1 (period 1), both observed for n ≤ 260. Hence W(E\_{t(nR)}) = +1 exactly when s(t(nR)) ≡ n (mod 2), and W(E\_{t(nR+T)}) = +1 exactly when s(t(nR + T)) is even. The data (primes ≥ 5 of F, S = split, N = non-split; exponent in brackets when > 1):

| member | t | primes ≥ 5 of F | s | f | W |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R | −1/3 | 1471 S | 1 | −1 | +1 |
| 2R | −15 | 859 N, 5743 S | 1 | +1 | −1 |
| 3R | 191/675 | 7 S \[2\], 19 S, 173 N, 233 S, (10 digits) N | 3 | −1 | +1 |
| 4R | 25665/36481 | 103 N, (27 digits) S | 1 | +1 | −1 |
| 5R | −17822401/8782563 | (9 digits) N, (36 digits) N | 0 | −1 | −1 |
| 2R + T | 11/12 | 1901 N, 65587 N | 0 | +1 | +1 |
| 3R + T | −104/121 | 7 N \[2\], 557 N, 1231 S, 120049 S | 2 | +1 | +1 |
| 4R + T | −28369/8112 | 19 S, 3457 S, 2852387 N, (16 digits) N | 2 | +1 | +1 |
| 5R + T | 3723720/6651241 | 171553 N, (11 digits) N, (26 digits) S | 1 | +1 | −1 |

The signs along nR are +, −, +, −, − and along nR + T they are +, +, +, − (for n = 2, …, 5), so no alternating pattern persists. As for C₂, the polynomial whose roots are the w-coordinates of the twelve points over the roots of the discriminant sextic 27w⁶ − 108w⁴ + 256w³ − 432w² + 1728 is irreducible of degree 12, and the number of its roots modulo the 3241 primes 7 ≤ p < 30000 follows the distribution of C₂⁶ ⋊ S₄ (frequencies 0.686, 0.189, 0.102, 0.012, 0.006, 0.004, 0.001 against 0.678, 0.191, 0.104, 0.013, 0.010, 0.004, 0.001). So the global root-number formula reduces the question on C₄′ to the same parity statement as on C₂ and does not imply infinitely many positive root numbers.

**Averaging the sign over the cover parameter (Heuristic; data Observed).** (i) *Balance over all t.* For t = a/b with |a| ≤ H and 1 ≤ b ≤ H the formula of §4 gives (total, W = +1, W = −1) = (39, 22, 17), (127, 67, 60), (511, 248, 263), (1111, 526, 585) and (3095, 1531, 1564) for H = 5, 10, 20, 30, 50, identical to the counts of an independent Magma computation of the root numbers (average sign −0.011 and positive fraction 0.495 at H = 50), and W(E\_t) = W(E\_{−t}) in 785 of 1547 pairs (0.507), so there is no visible pairing between t and −t. (ii) *The extra parameter does not enter the sign.* The pairs (t, s) with a rational point of E\_t of abscissa x\_s(t), or of abscissa c for the covers C\_c, are exactly the pairs (t, P) with P ∈ E\_t(ℚ), that is the rational points of the elliptic surface S. The sign W(E\_t) depends only on t, through the same Liouville-type factor of the same sextic form (§6 and Appendix B), so averaging over s changes the measure on t but not the arithmetic problem. (iii) *With s unrestricted the target is false, not just unproved.* Then every t with a rational point is covered. Under the parity conjecture these t include all those with W = −1, about half of all t, together with those of even rank ≥ 2; the proportion of W = +1 among them is the density of rank ≥ 2 among positive-rank t, which is 0 by the minimalist heuristics for a family of generic rank 0. A positive limiting proportion (liminf > 0) is therefore expected only for a thin set forced to carry a point, such as the members of one cover C\_c or C^{(s)} (where rank 1 and rank 2 are each expected with probability about 1/2). (iv) *With s bounded the sets are thin* (about (log H)^{1/2} members up to height H for each cover) and the question returns to the sign along a single sequence. (v) *Degrees.* After any parametrization t = P(u, v)/Q(u, v), every irreducible factor of the pulled-back discriminant has degree divisible by 6 (§6), so the unconditional averaged results for forms of degree ≤ 3 do not apply.

**Local state against global parity (Observed).** Write W(E\_t) = f(t)·Π(t) with the local factor f(t) = −w₂(t)w₃(t)(2/c) and the global factor Π(t) = ∏\_{p | F, p ≥ 5}(−(ab/p)) = (−1)^{s(t)}. For the 3095 rational t = a/b with |a|, b ≤ 50, f = +1 for 1973 of them (63.7%) and f = −1 for 1122, so the local factor is biased, while W is balanced (positive fraction 0.495). The fraction of Π = +1 is 0.495 among the t with f = +1 and 0.505 among those with f = −1, and within each of the eight classes of (w₂, w₃, (2/c)) it lies between 0.41 and 0.57 (the extreme values come from the classes with 39 and 44 curves; the classes with at least 455 curves lie between 0.49 and 0.52). So the local state and the global parity appear independent, and the balance of the sign comes entirely from Π. Along the sequences nP and nR of the covers the local state is not a mixing process but a deterministic periodic orbit in a finite quotient of the group of points (period 1 or 2 for f along nP, nR and 18 along nP + T), so the content of the problem lies in Π alone.

**Pairs (t, −t): the Liouville factor cancels (Proved identity, Observed accuracy of the factor-free form).** Since F(−t) = F(t) and c(−t) = c(t), E\_t and E\_{−t} have the same primes of multiplicative reduction and the same hypotenuse. For p ≥ 5 the local factors of W(E\_t)W(E\_{−t}) are χ\_p(t) = w\_p(t)w\_p(−t) = (−(ab/p))(−(−ab/p)) = (−1/p) if p | F, χ\_p(t) = 1 if p | c, and the archimedean signs cancel, so W(E\_t)W(E\_{−t}) = w₂(t)w₂(−t)w₃(t)w₃(−t)·∏ (−1/p), the product over the primes p ≥ 5 dividing F. The first factor depends only on (A, B) modulo 16 and modulo 9, and the second is (−1)^k with k the number of primes p ≡ 3 (mod 4) dividing F. This is the root number of E\_t over ℚ(i), because E\_{−t} is the twist of E\_t by −1, and it involves no Liouville function of the values of F. For squarefree F″ = |F|/(2^α3^β) one has ∏(−1/p) = (−1/F″) = (−1)^{(F″−1)/2}, so the root number of E\_t over ℚ(i) is then a periodic function of t. On the 979 pairs t = a/b, 1 ≤ a, b ≤ 40, the identity holds in 979 of 979 cases, and the form with (−1/F″) in place of ∏(−1/p) holds in 938 of 979 (95.8%; it fails when a prime ≡ 3 mod 4 divides F to an even power). Along C₂ the products W(t)W(−t) are −, −, −, +, −, −, +, +, −, −, − for t = −1, 3/4, −7, −40/9, 31/49, −561/400, 161/961, 34440/34969, −69409/529, −2047837/2965284, −917447/5013121, with no constant pattern. This does not give the sign of a single E\_t, because W(E\_t) = f(t)(−1)^{s(t)} still contains the Liouville-type factor, but it shows that W(E\_t) and W(E\_{−t}) are determined by one common unknown bit. It would give a rank statement for a pair (t, −t) both carrying a known point: if the product is −1, one of the two curves has W = +1 and a point, hence rank ≥ 2 (or Sha infinite). The covers considered here do not contain such pairs: C\_c(−t) is the twist by −1 of C\_c(t), and the only odd twists d(−t) = −d(t) with sections, d = 2t(t² − 1) and −t(1 + t²), have covers of rank 0. For the involutions of the covers that come from translation by a 2-torsion point (τ(t) = (t + 1)/(t − 1) on C₂ and τ(t) = −(1 + 3t)/(3(1 − t)) on C₄′) the singular fibres are not permuted (the numerator of D(τ(t)) is coprime to D), so the primes of F(t) and F(τ(t)) are unrelated and the Liouville factors do not cancel.

**Pairs (t, M(t)) for the torsion involution M (Proved for the primes 2, 5, 13, 17; Observed on 1110 pairs).** Let M(t) = (10 − 11t)/(10t + 11), the involution induced by translation by the 2-torsion point of the rank-2 Jacobian of C\_{−11/5} (M(M(t)) = t). On the circle t = tan(θ/2) it is the reflection θ ↦ 2β − θ with tan β = 10/11, that is (A, B) ↦ ((21A + 220B)/221, (220A − 21B)/221), and the hypotenuse is multiplied by 221 = 13·17; it is the composition of t ↦ −t with the rotation by the angle (21, 220, 221), and it reproduces the large primes 891481, 16931, 300916397, 2003 and 1114721 of the partners M(0), M(−7), M(−4), M(−2) and M(2) in an independent report. Writing χ\_p(t) = W\_p(E\_t)W\_p(E\_{M(t)}), over the 1110 pairs with t = u/v, |u|, v ≤ 30 one finds χ₂ = −1, χ₅ = +1, χ₁₃ = −1 and χ₁₇ = +1 in all 1110 cases, while χ₃ = −1 in 183 cases and +1 in 927. These four values hold for every rational t: (i) at p = 2, the local sign w₂ depends only on (A, B) modulo 16; 221 ≡ 13 is a unit with inverse 5, so (A, B) ↦ (9A + 12B, 12A + 7B) modulo 16, and a check of the 16 attainable classes shows w₂(M(t)) = −w₂(t) for all of them; (ii) at p = 5, the reflection is (A, −B) modulo 5, so E\_{M(t)} agrees locally with the twist of E\_t by −1, which is a square in ℚ₅, and the local signs agree; (iii) at p = 13 and 17 the hypotenuse of M(t) acquires one more factor p than that of t, giving the local sign (2/13) = −1 and (2/17) = +1. (An earlier version of this paragraph used the rotation by the same angle instead of M and reported χ₂ as non-constant; that was the wrong map.) Since χ₂χ₁₃ = +1, these constants do not affect the global sign. The remaining factor ∏\_{p ∉ {2, 5, 13, 17}} χ\_p(t) is not a character sum without Liouville factors: M does not permute the six singular fibres (the numerator of D(M(t)) is coprime to D; the resultant of F(t, 1) and G(t, 1), where G(a, b) = F(10b − 11a, 10a + 11b), is 2²⁰5⁶·7·239²·6133²·20873²·3532913·585807239²), so the primes dividing F(t) and F(M(t)) are unrelated, and the product is the product of two independent copies of the Liouville-type factor Π of §6.

**Common prime factors along the reflection pairs of C\_{−11/5} (Proved; Observed for n ≤ 20).** Let P be a generator of the rank-2 group of C\_{−11/5} and M(t) = (10 − 11t)/(10t + 11) the translation by 2-torsion. A computation with exact integers shows gcd(F″(t(nP)), F″(t(nP + T))) = 1 for 0 ≤ n ≤ 20 (the values reach 8000 digits). A prime p ≥ 5 dividing both F(a, b) and G(a, b) = F(10b − 11a, 10a + 11b) for primitive (a, b) must divide Res(F(t, 1), G(t, 1)) = 2²⁰5⁶·7·239²·6133²·20873²·3532913·585807239², and in fact must have a common root of F and G in the projective line over 𝔽\_p. There is none for p = 5, 13, 17, 239, 6133, 20873. For p = 7 (common root t = 4) and p = 585807239 (two common roots) the fibre of C\_{−11/5} over the root has no 𝔽\_p-point (w² = (1 + t²)q\_c(t) is a non-residue), so no rational point of the cover reduces to it, and these primes never divide both. For p = 3532913 the common root t = 3198986 has 𝔽\_p-points, M fixes it modulo p, and the point B of the cover over t = −201/43 (w < 0, which has the heights of a generator of the lattice) has order 882828 modulo p with t(nB) ≡ 3198986 and t(nB + T) ≡ 3198986, both roots of F, for n ≡ 20062 and n ≡ 461476 (mod 882828) (checked by modular arithmetic). So 3532913 divides both F″ values at those n, the first being n = 20062, and the coprimality observed for n ≤ 20 does not hold for all n; for every generator of the lattice, residue classes of n with this property exist whenever the relevant points lie in the cyclic subgroup it generates modulo p.

## Appendix C. A method: reducing statements about all multiples of a point to finite computations

This appendix records a method that was used several times in the paper (the periods of the local signs, the common prime factors of two sextic values, the fixed-prime lemma for the reflection pairs) in a form that can be reused.

**Setting.** Let J be an elliptic curve over ℚ, P ∈ J(ℚ) a point of infinite order, and t : J → ℙ¹ a rational function defined over ℚ (here a degree-2 function, t = 1 − z on y² = z³ − 2z² + 2z, or the corresponding function on the Jacobian of C\_{−11/5}). For n with t(nP) finite write t(nP) = a\_n/b\_n in lowest terms. Let F and G be forms with integer coefficients (here F(a, b) = a⁶ − 30a⁴b² − 24a²b⁴ − b⁶ and G the form F composed with the Möbius involution induced by translation by a 2-torsion point).

**Lemma (reduction to finitely many primes and a finite orbit).** Let p be a prime at which J has good reduction, and let m\_p be the order of P modulo p. If p divides both F(a\_n, b\_n) and G(a\_n, b\_n), then (i) p divides the homogeneous resultant Res(F, G), and (ii) t(nP) mod p is a common root of F and G in ℙ¹(𝔽\_p). Both conditions depend only on n modulo m\_p, because (n + m\_p)P ≡ nP (mod p). Hence "gcd(F(a\_n, b\_n), G(a\_n, b\_n)) has no prime factor outside a given finite set for every n" is decided by finitely many orbit computations.

*Proof.* If p divides F(a, b) and G(a, b) with (a, b) primitive, the point (a : b) of ℙ¹(𝔽\_p) is a common zero of F and G, so the two forms have a common factor modulo p and p divides their resultant. The orbit of the reduction of P is periodic with period m\_p ≤ p + 1 + 2√p, and t(nP) mod p depends only on nP mod p. □

**Algorithm.**

1. Compute and factor R = Res(F(X, 1), G(X, 1)) (and treat the points at infinity separately: here F(1, 0) = 1, so ∞ is not a root of F). The primes dividing R, together with the primes of bad reduction of J and the primes dividing the determinant of the Möbius map, form the finite exceptional set.
2. For each exceptional prime p, factor F and G modulo p and list the common roots r in 𝔽\_p.
3. (Cheap exclusion.) If the fibre of the cover over t = r has no 𝔽\_p-point (the quadratic character of the right-hand side of the cover equation at t = r is −1), no rational point of the cover reduces to r and p is excluded for all n.
4. (Orbit test.) Otherwise compute the orbit nP mod p for n = 1, …, m\_p − 1 and record the residue classes of n with t(nP) ≡ r.
5. If no class is recorded for any exceptional prime, the two values are coprime for every n; otherwise the recorded classes (with their modulus m\_p) describe exactly the n at which the prime divides both.

**Results.** On C₂ (J₂ : y² = z³ − 2z² + 2z, P = (1, 1)) the exceptional primes are 7, 103 and 519521 with orders of P equal to 6, 54 and 698 and no hits, so the two sextic values are coprime for every n. On C\_{−11/5} the exceptional prime 3532913 has orbit length 882828 for the point B over t = −201/43 and hits the common root t = 3198986 at n ≡ 20062 and 461476 (mod 882828), so the analogous statement is false there.

**Variant for translates: the pair test (step 4′).** To decide whether a prime p can divide the values of F at nP and at (n + k)P, collect the finite set S of points of J(𝔽\_p) lying over the roots of F in 𝔽\_p (at most 12 points). A pair R₁, R₂ ∈ S with R₂ = R₁ + kP is necessary. This needs no orbit, so it works for primes of any size: at p = 2325076196570957, where the orbit of P has length of order 10¹⁵ and cannot be walked, the test takes 0.0 s. It is necessary but not sufficient: if a pair exists one must still decide whether R₁ lies in the subgroup generated by P (by walking the orbit when p is small, or by a discrete logarithm), as was done for 47, 18637 and 36997 in Proposition D3 (Appendix D), where it does not. The code below is the pair test for k = 2 on C₂.

```python
from sympy import sqrt_mod
from sympy.polys.galoistools import gf_factor, gf_from_int_poly, gf_degree
from sympy.polys.domains import ZZ

def roots_f(p):                       # roots in F_p of t^6 - 30 t^4 - 24 t^2 - 1
    g = gf_from_int_poly([1, 0, -30, 0, -24, 0, -1], p)
    lc, facs = gf_factor(g, p, ZZ)
    return sorted((-gg[1]) % p for gg, m in facs if gf_degree(gg) == 1)

def add(P, Q, p):                     # group law on y^2 = x^3 - 2 x^2 + 2 x over F_p
    if P is None: return Q
    if Q is None: return P
    (x1, y1), (x2, y2) = P, Q
    if x1 == x2:
        if (y1 + y2) % p == 0: return None
        lam = (3*x1*x1 - 4*x1 + 2) * pow(2*y1, -1, p) % p
    else:
        lam = (y2 - y1) * pow(x2 - x1, -1, p) % p
    x3 = (lam*lam + 2 - x1 - x2) % p
    return (x3, (lam*(x1 - x3) - y1) % p)

def pair_test(p):                     # pairs R1, R2 = R1 + 2P over roots of f (t = 1 - x)
    S = set()
    for r in roots_f(p):
        x = (1 - r) % p
        v = (x**3 - 2*x*x + 2*x) % p
        if v == 0: S.add((x, 0)); continue
        s = sqrt_mod(v, p)
        if s is not None: S |= {(x, s), (x, (-s) % p)}
    two_P = (pow(4, -1, p), (-5 * pow(8, -1, p)) % p)
    return [(R, add(R, two_P, p)) for R in S if add(R, two_P, p) in S]
```

**Other uses in this paper.** The same idea, that a local quantity along nP depends only on nP modulo a prime power and is therefore periodic, underlies the periodicity of the finite part f(t\\\_n) of the root number (proved for p = 3, period 18 for nP + T; verified for p = 2 up to n = 534, not proved by this method), and the finite residue checks behind the constancy of the characters χ\\\_p of the involution M at p = 2, 5, 13, 17. Those checks were done by different scripts than the one below, and this appendix does not reprove them.

**Limits.** The method decides statements about finitely many primes. It says nothing about the number of prime factors of the values, so it does not touch the Liouville-parity problem H. The orbit test costs the orbit length, which can be as large as the prime itself (about 3.5·10⁶ steps was easy, about 6·10⁸ would need compiled code); the pair test of step 4′ avoids this cost and decides most primes at once and it needs good reduction at the primes tested and care with primitive normalizations of the coordinates (here the common divisors of the reflected coordinates divide 2, 13 and 17 and are handled separately).

**Code (Python with sympy).** The script below reproduces the C₂ computation and can be adapted to another cover by changing the curve equation, the point and the map t.

```python
from sympy import symbols, Poly, expand, resultant, factorint, legendre_symbol
from sympy.polys.galoistools import gf_factor, gf_from_int_poly, gf_degree
from sympy.polys.domains import ZZ

a, b, t = symbols('a b t')
F = lambda x, y: x**6 - 30*x**4*y**2 - 24*x**2*y**4 - y**6
G2 = expand(F(a + b, a - b))              # the involution (a, b) -> (a + b, a - b) of C2

Fc = [int(c) for c in Poly(F(t, 1), t).all_coeffs()]
Gc = [int(c) for c in Poly(G2.subs({a: t, b: 1}), t).all_coeffs()]
Res = resultant(Poly(F(t, 1), t).as_expr(), Poly(G2.subs({a: t, b: 1}), t).as_expr(), t)
print("Res =", factorint(int(Res)))

def linear_roots(co, p):
    f = gf_from_int_poly(co, p)
    lc, facs = gf_factor(f, p, ZZ)
    return sorted((-g[1]) % p for g, m in facs if gf_degree(g) == 1)

def orbit_z(p):
    # y^2 = z^3 - 2 z^2 + 2 z over F_p with P = (1, 1): returns z(nP) for n = 1, ..., m_p - 1
    def add(P, Q):
        if P is None: return Q
        if Q is None: return P
        x1, y1 = P; x2, y2 = Q
        if x1 == x2:
            if (y1 + y2) % p == 0: return None
            lam = (3*x1*x1 - 4*x1 + 2) * pow(2*y1, -1, p) % p
        else:
            lam = (y2 - y1) * pow(x2 - x1, -1, p) % p
        x3 = (lam*lam + 2 - x1 - x2) % p
        return (x3, (lam*(x1 - x3) - y1) % p)
    P = (1, 1); Q = P; zs = [1]
    while True:
        Q = add(Q, P)
        if Q is None: break
        zs.append(Q[0])
    return zs

for p in [q for q in factorint(int(Res)) if q >= 5]:
    common = sorted(set(linear_roots(Fc, p)) & set(linear_roots(Gc, p)))
    zs = orbit_z(p)
    order = len(zs) + 1
    hits = [n + 1 for n, z in enumerate(zs) if (1 - z) % p in common]
    for r in common:
        val = ((1 - r) * (1 + r*r)) % p            # cover C2: w^2 = (1 - t)(1 + t^2)
        print("p =", p, "root", r, "cover has points above it:",
              val == 0 or legendre_symbol(val, p) == 1)
    print("p =", p, "order of P =", order, "common roots =", common, "hits =", hits or "none")
```

## Appendix D. Reflection, translation by 2P and doubling: sorted register of imported results

This appendix sorts the results of two research files dated 5 November 2026 (a differential update on reflection and doubling, and an addendum on the sextic character fields) against the rest of the paper. Every item was re-derived in exact arithmetic unless marked otherwise. Notation: f(t) = t⁶ − 30t⁴ − 24t² − 1, K = ℚ(α) with f(α) = 0, M(t) = (t + 1)/(t − 1), c(t) = −6t(1 + t²), s(t) = (−2t⁵ + 60t³ + 46t − 4)/3, h = 3s. For t = a/b in lowest terms put u = M(t) = c/d in lowest terms (so (c, d) = (a + b, a − b)/g with g ∈ {1, 2}), C\_t = −6ab(a² + b²), C\_u = −6cd(c² + d²), and let R\_t, R\_u be the products of the primes p ≥ 5 dividing F(a, b) and F(c, d).

### D.1 Proved (re-derived here)

| # | Statement | Basis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | −c(M(α)) = s(α)² in K, i.e. s(t)²(t − 1)³ ≡ 12(t + 1)(t² + 1) mod f, and Res(f, h) = −186624 = −2⁸3⁶. | remainder 0, resultant recomputed |
| 2 | For every prime p ≥ 5 and every root r of f in 𝔽\_p, (c(M(r))/p) = (−1/p). | from 1: s(r) ≠ 0 mod p because Res(f, h) is a unit; also 150 of 150 roots for 5 ≤ p < 1000 (the source says 148; the difference is only which primes were counted) |
| 3 | C\_tC\_u = 2ab(a² − b²)·(6(a² + b²)/g²)². | identity of polynomials |
| 4 | **Theorem D1 (reflected Jacobi identity, all t).** (C\_t/R\_t)(C\_u/R\_u) = (−2ab(a² − b²)/(R\_tR\_u)). | proof below; 4405 of 4405 cases with |
| 5 | 2P = (1/4, −5/8) and t(Q + 2P) = (4(3x − 7)(4x + 1) − 80y)/(4(4x − 1)²) for Q = (x, y) on y² = x³ − 2x² + 2x with 4x ≠ 1. | checked for Q = nP, n = 1, 3, …, 12 |
| 6 | Res\_x(f(1 − x), N(x)) = 2¹⁸⁶3²⁷23¹²47²947²4241¹²18637·36997²·2325076196570957² with deg N = 24, and Res\_x(f(1 − x), 4x − 1) = −97543 = −23·4241. | exponents and the trivial cofactor recomputed |
| 7 | The doubling map is 𝒟(t) = (t⁴ − 2t² + 8t − 3)/(4(t − 1)(t² + 1)), t\_{2n} = 𝒟(t\_n) (n = 1, …, 8); H = U⁶ − 30U⁴V² − 24U²V⁴ − V⁶ has degree 24, is irreducible over ℚ, has constant term −97543, gcd(f, H) = 1, and Res(f, H) = 2³⁶·19·229·797·7351·12113·363773·609613·9624457993·1386618529213. | recomputed |
| 8 | N\_{K/ℚ}(γ) = −148782798566901582531/2³³ for γ = c(𝒟(α)); hence γ and −γ are non-squares in K, \[K(√γ) : K\] = 2 and γ ∉ ℚ^×K^{×2}. | recomputed; the negative sign is the whole argument, since N(ds²) = d⁶N(s)² > 0 |
| 9 | c\_1 = c(α), c\_2 = c(M(α)) = −s², c\_3 = c\_1c\_2 are non-squares in K; \[c\_2\] = \[−1\], K(√c\_2) = K(i), \[L : K\] = 4 and \[L : ℚ\] = 24 for L = K(√c\_1, i); σ(α) = −α sends c\_1 to −c\_1, so σ\[c\_1\] = \[c\_3\] and K(√c\_1) ≅ K(√c\_3) over ℚ. | N(c\_1) = (−6)⁶·N(α)·N(1 + α²) = 46656·(−1)·64 < 0 shows c\_1 and −c\_1 are non-squares; −1 is not a square because f has real roots |

**Proof of Theorem D1.** (i) For p ≥ 5 dividing F(a, b): F ≡ −b⁶ if p | a, F ≡ a⁶ if p | b, F ≡ −8b⁶ if p | a² + b², and F(1, 1) = −54 excludes p | a − b, so p ∤ ab(a² + b²)(a − b) and (C\_t/p) ≠ 0. (ii) Since C\_u = d⁴c(M(t)) up to the factor g⁴, (C\_u/p) = (c(M(r))/p) = (−1/p) for r = a/b mod p, by item 2. Exchanging the roles of t and u (M is an involution) gives (C\_t/p) = (−1/p) for p | R\_u. (iii) For Jacobi symbols (n/mk) = (n/m)(n/k) holds always, so (C\_tC\_u/(R\_tR\_u)) = (C\_t/R\_t)(C\_u/R\_u)(C\_t/R\_u)(C\_u/R\_t) = (C\_t/R\_t)(C\_u/R\_u)(−1/R\_tR\_u); no coprimality of R\_t and R\_u is used. (iv) By item 3, C\_tC\_u is 2ab(a² − b²) times a square coprime to R\_tR\_u, because p ∤ a² + b² for p | R\_t and p ∤ c² + d² for p | R\_u. Combining gives the identity. □

The hypotheses that the source files list as outstanding (coprimality, denominators, local reduction) are therefore not needed for Theorem D1. What they leave open is the 2- and 3-adic part, which is the finite factor f(t)f(M(t)) already controlled in §4.

**Corollary D2 (pair of root numbers).** At a prime p ≥ 5 dividing F(a, b) one has (ab/p) = (C\_t/p) (2240 of 2240 pairs on |u|, v ≤ 30, and by 27b²c ≡ −4a³ together with c ≡ −4a³/(27b²) in the triple of §4), so W(E\_t) = f(t)(−1)^{ω(R\_t)}(C\_t/R\_t) and

W(E\_t)W(E\_{M(t)}) = f(t)f(M(t))·(−1)^{ω(R\_t) + ω(R\_u)}·(−2ab(a² − b²)/(R\_tR\_u)).

On C₂ the two sextic values are coprime (Appendix C), so ω(R\_tR\_u) = ω(R\_t) + ω(R\_u) and, if the product N(F·G₂) is squarefree, the pair product is f(t)f(M(t))·λ(R)·(−2ab(a² − b²)/R) with R = R\_tR\_u: one Liouville value of the degree-12 form F·G₂ and one explicit Jacobi symbol. This reorganizes the correlation Σ λ(N\_n)λ(N\_n′) of the main text but does not control λ(R); it is not a distribution result.

**Proposition D3 (translation by 2P; Proved by a finite computation).** For every n ≥ 1, gcd(N(F(a\_n, b\_n)), N(F(a\_{n+2}, b\_{n+2}))) = 1, where t\_n = a\_n/b\_n = 1 − x(nP) in lowest terms and N(m) is the part of |m| prime to 6. Proof. A prime p ≥ 5 dividing both values has t\_n and t\_{n+2} among the roots of f modulo p. Then x = x(nP) satisfies f(1 − x) ≡ 0 together with N(x) ≡ 0 (item 6) or 4x ≡ 1 (the degenerate case Q = ±2P), so p divides Res(f(1 − x), N(x)) or Res(f(1 − x), 4x − 1) = −97543, that is p lies in {23, 47, 947, 4241, 18637, 36997, 2325076196570957}. The pair test of Appendix C (step 4′) excludes four of them outright: for p = 23, 4241 and 2325076196570957 there is no pair of points R₁, R₂ = R₁ + 2P over roots of f, and for p = 947 the two roots of f have no point of J(𝔽\_p) above them. For p = 47, 18637 and 36997 pairs exist (2, 1 and 2 of them), but the orbit of P has order 7, 9368 and 18336 modulo p and contains no n for which both nP and (n + 2)P lie over roots of f, so these primes never divide both values. □ Checks: a scan of all primes 5 ≤ p < 20000 finds pairs only at 47 and 18637, as the candidate list predicts, and the exact gcd for 1 ≤ n ≤ 60 is 1 in every case.

### D.2 Matches with the rest of the paper

The nine signs W(E\_{t\_n}) = (+, −, −, −, −, +, +, −, +) for n = 1, …, 9 agree with the table of Appendix B (t\_1 = 0 is the torsion member with W = +1), and the nine products W(E\_{t\_n})W(E\_{M(t\_n)}) = (−, −, +, +, +, −, +, +, +) agree with the products of the two columns nP and nP + T of that table for n = 2, …, 9 (t = −1 gives −1 at n = 1). The doubling products D\_n = ε\_nε\_{2n} = (−, +, −, +) for n = 1, …, 4 and the lag correlations C\_1, …, C\_5 = 0, −1/7, 0, −3/5, 0 were recomputed. All of these are observations on short samples: D\_n = (−1)ⁿ for n ≤ 4 and the C\_m prove nothing about a limit.

### D.3 Candidates and open items

- **Translation by 2P (item 6).** The primes 23, 47, 947, 4241, 18637, 36997 and 2325076196570957 were the candidates for common divisors of the discriminant values at Q and Q + 2P. All seven are now excluded (Proposition D3), so the two values are coprime for every n.
- **Doubling (items 7, 8).** The scope of item 8 is limited: it excludes only a class in ℚ^×K^{×2}. A relation of \[γ\] with classes such as \[c\_1\] or other elements of K is not excluded and has not been tested. The characters of K(√γ) are not local factors of the root number of the doubled curve, whose bad primes come from H.
- **Automorphisms.** The reported fact Aut\_ℚ(K) = {1, σ} was not recomputed; the cubic resolvent y³ − 30y² − 24y − 1 has discriminant 3⁹·23, not a square, which is consistent with it. Only the existence of σ is used above.

### D.4 Not established or rejected

- **W\_p(E\_t) = −(−1/p) for p | F.** Not proved and not needed. The proved local sign at p | F(t) is −(ab/p) = −(C\_t/p) (§4). The reported test (78 of 148 agreements) compared the character at the reflected parameter with the local sign of the other curve, at primes that need not divide that curve's discriminant, so its failure does not contradict anything in this paper.
- **A prime-modulo-4 cancellation rule** for the combined multiplicative contribution: matched 6 of 9 cases (failed at n = 2, 6, 8). Rejected.
- **A universal square-class identity for doubling** of the reflection type with a rational constant: false by item 8, but see its scope above.

## Appendix E. The 2-isogeny and the Galois groups of the sextic and of the character extension: sorted register

This appendix sorts a research file on the 2-isogeny of J₂ and on the splitting fields of f and of R(z) = Res\_t(f(t), z² − g(t)), with g(t) = 2t(1 − t²). Everything marked *recomputed* was verified here in exact arithmetic. Notation: F(x) = f(1 − x), N the splitting field of f, M the splitting field of R, a₁, a₂, a₃ one root from each pair ±a\_i of roots of f.

### E.1 Recomputed

1. F(x) = x⁶ − 6x⁵ − 15x⁴ + 100x³ − 189x² + 162x − 54; G(x) = x⁶F(2/x) = −54x⁶ + 324x⁵ − 756x⁴ + 800x³ − 240x² − 192x + 64; gcd(F, G) = 1. So the root divisor of F is not preserved by x ↦ 2/x and F does not descend.
2. The isogeny J₂ → J₂′ : Y² = X³ + 4X² − 4X is X = y²/x² = x + 2/x − 2.
3. F(x)F(2/x) = H₆(X) with H₆ = −54X⁶ + 1998X⁴ − 4060X³ + 2730X² − 624X + 64, irreducible over ℚ. Hence for a root x = 1 − α of F the value β = X(x) has degree 6, Q(β) = Q(α), and the isogeny gives another generator of the same sextic field, not a smaller one.
4. R(z) = z¹² − 109188z⁸ − 991440z⁴ − 186624, irreducible over ℚ; R(0) = −186624 = N\_{ℚ(α)/ℚ}(g(α)) = −2⁸3⁶, so g(α) is a unit away from 2 and 3.
5. **Gal(N/ℚ) ≅ S₄, of order 24 (proved).** Write f(t) = h(t²) with h(y) = y³ − 30y² − 24y − 1, irreducible with discriminant 3⁹·23, not a square, so the Galois group of h is S₃. The roots satisfy y₁y₂y₃ = 1, hence √y₁√y₂√y₃ = ±1 and the group of f lies in {ε ∈ C₂³ : ∏ε\_i = 1} ⋊ S₃ ≅ V₄ ⋊ S₃ ≅ S₄. The subgroup V₄ ∩ Gal is S₃-invariant, so it is 0 or V₄; it is not 0, because a Frobenius element of cycle type (2, 4) occurs (below) and S₃ has no element of order 4. So Gal(N/ℚ) = S₄ and \[N : ℚ\] = 24. The cycle types of f modulo p for the 33857 primes 5 ≤ p < 4·10⁵ (p ≠ 23) are (1,1,2,2): 0.3774, (3,3): 0.3334, (2,4): 0.2483, (1⁶): 0.0409, against 9/24, 8/24, 6/24, 1/24 predicted for S₄ on the six roots, and no other type occurs.
6. The pairs ±a\_i: since g is odd, g(−a) = −g(a), so \[g(−a)\] = \[−1\]\[g(a)\] and (g(−a)/p) = (−1/p)(g(a)/p). The roots of R are ±√g(a\_i) and ±i√g(a\_i), so M = N(i, √g(a₁), √g(a₂), √g(a₃)) and \[M : N\] ≤ 16, that is |Gal(M/ℚ)| ≤ 384 instead of the naive 24·2⁶ = 1536. (The two displayed products g(a₁)g(−a₁)g(a₂)g(−a₂) and g(a₁)g(−a₁)g(a₃)g(−a₃) are squares for the trivial reason g(a)g(−a) = −g(a)²; that they generate all relations is equivalent to |Gal(M/ℚ)| = 384.)
7. N ⊂ M: S(w) = Res\_t(f(t), w − g(t)) is irreducible (R(z) = S(z²) is), so ℚ(g(α)) = ℚ(α).

### E.2 Independence of the square classes and the order 384 (proved; Chebotarev check)

The order |Gal(R/ℚ)| = 384, that is \[M : N\] = 16, was reported from Magma; it is proved in the paragraph after the next, and the statistics below are a consistency check. The lower bound needs i ∉ N, which holds because the only quadratic subfield of N is ℚ(√69), and the independence of \[g(a₁)\], \[g(a₂)\], \[g(a₃)\] modulo squares and −1. A Chebotarev check supports it: of the 1386 primes below 4·10⁵ that split completely in N (fraction 0.0409 against 1/24), R splits completely at 89 (0.0642 against 1/16 = 0.0625), and for the 683 of them with p ≡ 1 (mod 4) the number of residues among the three symbols (g(a\_i)/p) is distributed as 84, 258, 252, 89 against 85, 256, 256, 85 for independence.

**Proposition E (|Gal(M/ℚ)| = 384; proved).** The classes \[g(a₁)\], \[g(a₂)\], \[g(a₃)\], \[−1\] are independent in N^×/N^{×2}, hence \[M : N\] = 16 by Kummer theory and |Gal(M/ℚ)| = 24·16 = 384. *Proof.* Let V = 𝔽₂⁴ with basis e₁, e₂, e₃, ε mapping to \[g(a₁)\], \[g(a₂)\], \[g(a₃)\], \[−1\], and let K ⊂ V be the kernel of this map to N^×/N^{×2}. The group Gal(N/ℚ) = C₂²⋊S₃ ≅ S₄ acts on V: S₃ permutes the e\_i, an even sign change flipping the roots a\_i, i ∈ S, sends e\_i to e\_i + ε for i ∈ S (because g(−a) = −g(a)), and ε is fixed. K is stable under this action. A computation (enumerating the subspaces of 𝔽₂⁴ closed under the generators) shows that V has exactly 7 submodules, and that the only one containing none of ε, σ = e₁ + e₂ + e₃ and σ + ε is 0. Now ε ∉ K because −1 is not a square in N, and σ, σ + ε ∉ K: the product of the six values of g is Res(f, g) = −2⁸3⁶ = −(g(a₁)g(a₂)g(a₃))² (as g(a)g(−a) = −g(a)²), so g(a₁)g(a₂)g(a₃) = ±432 = ±3·12², whose class is \[±3\], and \[−g(a₁)g(a₂)g(a₃)\] = \[∓3\]. Since Gal(N/ℚ) = S₄ has a unique subgroup of index 2, ℚ(√69) is the only quadratic subfield of N, so none of −1, 3, −3 is a square in N. Hence K = 0. □ In particular M = N(i, √g(a₁), √g(a₂), √g(a₃)) has degree 16 over N (the roots of R are ±√g(a\_i) and ±i√g(a\_i)), and the field identification N ⊂ M is item E.1.7 (ℚ(g(α)) = ℚ(α) because S is irreducible), so no further field-identification step remains.

### E.3 Relation to the rest of the paper

The expression g(t) = 2t(1 − t²) is the t-form of the product ab of the legs of the triple, so the symbols (g(a)/p) at the roots a of f modulo p are the local signs at the primes p | F, w\_p = −(g(a)/p) (§4). Moreover −3(1 + α²)/(1 − α²) = c(α)/g(α), with c(t) = −6t(1 + t²) as in Appendix D, is a square in ℚ(α): it is a square modulo p at all 5938 roots of f in 𝔽\_p for p < 60000. Hence \[g(α)\] = \[c(α)\] and M contains the field L = K(√c₁, i) of Appendix D (degree 24 over ℚ). The question asked at the end of the research file, whether the prime-divisor character product in the root-number formula can be handled by reciprocity, is answered by the Hilbert-symbol reduction of Appendix B: along nP the product of the local signs at the primes dividing F\_n reduces to local symbols at 2, 3 and ∞ and is periodic of period 6 (proved). In Chebotarev terms each individual sign w\_p is equidistributed over Gal(M/ℚ), but along nP the root a(p, n) is fixed by n modulo the order of P, which is why no distribution statement follows.

### E.4 What this does not give

None of this is a distribution theorem for the global root numbers, nor a step toward infinitely many specializations of rank at least 2: it constrains the characters at a fixed prime (the oddness of g and the evenness of f), and the remaining obstruction is the Liouville value λ(F″\_n) (Problem H).

### F.A The sieve, the Galois structure and the class relations (F.1–F.10)

**F.1 The sieve (Proved).** Since x(nP) is a rational square (Appendix B) and y² = (1 − t)(1 + t²) at every point, a prime p ≥ 5 can divide F\_n only if t\_n ≡ r (mod p) for a root r of f in 𝔽\_p with both 1 − r and 1 + r² nonzero squares in 𝔽\_p (f(1) = −54 and f(i) = −8 rule out vanishing). Let G\_p(t) = gcd(f, (1 − t)^{(p−1)/2} − 1, (1 + t²)^{(p−1)/2} − 1) in 𝔽\_p\[t\]; its roots are exactly the admissible roots, all in 𝔽\_p. If deg G\_p = 0 then p ∤ F\_n for every n (also when p | V\_n, since then F\_n ≡ U\_n⁶ ≢ 0). Recomputed for the 667 primes 5 ≤ p ≤ 5000: 397 have no root of f, 59 have roots but no square 1 − r, 93 have a square 1 − r but no admissible root, and 118 have an admissible root, that is 118 of the 270 primes with roots (0.437); of the 660 roots 328 pass the first condition and 173 pass both (0.262). The orbit of P modulo p meets the roots for 81 primes, 37 admissible primes are missed by the orbit, and no orbit hit violates the sieve.

**F.2 The Galois group of the closure of K = ℚ(r, √(1 − r), √(1 + r²)) (Proved).** Let L = N(√(1 − α), √(1 + α²) : f(α) = 0). Write A\_i⁺ = \[1 − a\_i\], A\_i⁻ = \[1 + a\_i\], B\_i = \[1 + a\_i²\] for the three pairs ±a\_i in N^×/N^{×2} (nine classes). *Relation.* −3(1 − α⁴) = s(α)² with s(t) = (t⁵ − 29t³ − 26t)/3 (verified exactly: s² + 3(1 − t⁴) ≡ 0 mod f), so A\_i⁺A\_i⁻B\_i = \[1 − a\_i⁴\] = \[−3\] for each i; two independent relations. This also proves the identity \[g(α)\] = \[c(α)\] of Appendix E, because c(t)/g(t) = −3(1 − t⁴)/(1 − t²)², which had been checked only at 5938 roots. *Rank.* Evaluating the nine symbols at all labelings (a₁a₂a₃ = 1, 24 per prime) for the 714 primes below 2·10⁵ that split completely in N gives 128 distinct vectors of rank 7, so at most two independent relations exist, and the two above are all of them. Hence the span has dimension 7, \[L : N\] = 2⁷, and |Gal(L/ℚ)| = 24·128 = 3072. Note that √−3 ∈ L but √−3 ∉ N (the only quadratic subfield of N is ℚ(√69)), so the character κ(p) = (−3/p) is independent of the Frobenius class in N.

**F.3 Density (Proved by Chebotarev; checked numerically).** Seven independent characters on Gal(L/N) are κ and, for each pair i, the symbols of 1 − a\_i and 1 + a\_i, with the symbol of 1 + a\_i² equal to κ times their product. A Frobenius element of type (1⁶) fixes all six roots; given κ the three pairs are independent and a pair is admissible (symbol of 1 + a² = +1 and at least one of 1 ∓ a a square) with probability 1/4 if κ = +1 and 1/2 if κ = −1, so at least one admissible root occurs with probability ½(1 − (3/4)³) + ½(1 − (1/2)³) = 93/128. A Frobenius of type (1,1,2,2) (nine of 24) fixes exactly one pair and gives probability ½·¼ + ½·½ = 3/8; other types fix no root. Hence the set of primes p for which f has an admissible root has natural density

(1/24)(93/128) + (9/24)(3/8) = 175/1024 ≈ 0.1709,
