with density 181/1536 ≈ 0.1178 among p ≡ 1 (mod 3) (κ = +1) and 43/192 ≈ 0.2240 among p ≡ 2 (mod 3) (κ = −1). For the 25994 primes 5 ≤ p < 3·10⁵ (p ≠ 23) the observed proportions are 1516/12970 = 0.1169, 2912/13024 = 0.2236 and 0.1703 overall; the model with nine free classes would give 0.1721 for both residue classes, so the asymmetry modulo 3 confirms the relation. So the primes that can divide some F\_n have upper density at most 175/1024.

**F.4 The second obstruction (heuristic).** An admissible root r gives a point R of J₂(𝔽\_p) with x(R) = 1 − r a nonzero square, i.e. R lies in the index-2 subgroup φ̂(J₂′(𝔽\_p)) that contains ⟨P⟩. The remaining condition R ∈ ⟨P⟩ is of Artin type (it depends on the index of ⟨P⟩ in that subgroup) and is not a Chebotarev condition in a fixed number field; among the 118 admissible primes below 5000, 81 are hit by the orbit and 37 are not.

**F.5 The compositum of the sieve field and the character field (Proved under the following reading of L\*).** Let L\* = L·M, where L is the field of F.2 and M = N(i, √g(a₁), √g(a₂), √g(a₃)) is the field of Appendix E, that is L\* = N(√(1 − a\_i), √(1 + a\_i), √(1 + a\_i²), √−1, √g(a\_i)). Then \[N : ℚ\] = 24, \[L : N\] = 2⁷, \[L\* : N\] = 2¹⁰ and \[L\* : L\] = 8, so |Gal(L\*/ℚ)| = 24·2¹⁰ = 24576. *Proof.* Over the 7-dimensional span of the nine classes of F.2 one adjoins the four classes \[−1\], \[g(a₁)\], \[g(a₂)\], \[g(a₃)\], an 11-element generating set. The only relation among them is \[g(a₁)g(a₂)g(a₃)\] = \[±432\] = \[±3\] = \[±1\]·\[−3\], with \[−3\] already in the span of F.2 (Appendix E: g(a₁)g(a₂)g(a₃) = ±432), so the rank is at most 7 + 4 − 1 = 10. Evaluating the symbols of the nine classes together with (−1/p) and (g(a\_i)/p) at all 24 labelings of the 714 primes below 2·10⁵ that split completely in N gives vectors of rank 10, so the rank is exactly 10. (Equivalently \[g(a\_i)\] = \[2\]\[a\_i\]\[1 − a\_i\]\[1 + a\_i\] with \[2\] = B₁B₂B₃ in the span, so over L the new classes are \[−1\] and \[a\_i\] with a₁a₂a₃ = 1; adjoining −1, 2, 3 alone raises the rank only to 8, and adjoining −1, 2, 3 and a₁, a₂, a₃ gives 10.) □ This is the field that carries both the sieve characters (the symbols of 1 − r and 1 + r²) and the local root-number characters (the symbols of g(r)). The new classes are independent of those of L, so the local sign −(g(r)/p) at an admissible root is equidistributed given admissibility: among the 4458 admissible roots below 2·10⁵, (g(r)/p) = +1 for 2265 (0.508) and = −1 for 2193, and the proportions are the same modulo 3 (1110 and 1106 for p ≡ 1, 1155 and 1087 for p ≡ 2). This is a statement about a fixed prime, not about the primes dividing F\_n along nP.

**F.6 The independent dossier on L\*: confirmation, the extra relation, and a correction (Proved; one formula corrected).** An independent dossier defines L = N(√A\_i, √B\_i, √C\_i) and L\* = L(√D\_i, √E\_i) with A\_i = 1 − a\_i, B\_i = 1 + a\_i, C\_i = 1 + a\_i², D\_i = 2a\_i, E\_i = −2a\_i, and reports \[L\* : N\] = 2¹⁰, \[L\* : L\] = 8 from Magma (exact number-field square tests, 2047 products, one extra relation). This is the same field as the L\* of F.5: D\_iE\_i = −4a\_i² gives \[E\_i\] = \[−1\]\[D\_i\], and g(a\_i) = D\_iA\_iB\_i with A\_iB\_i = \[−3\]C\_i in the span of L, so L(√−1, √g(a\_i)) = L(√D\_i, √E\_i). *Certificate.* With the consistent labelings (a₁a₂a₃ = +1, 24 per prime; the dossier's remark that independently sorted labels are not compatible across primes is correct, and the diagnostic rank 11 it mentions is not a lower bound) the symbol vectors of the nine old classes have rank 7 and those of all fifteen classes A, B, C, D, E have rank 10 (560 primes below 1.5·10⁵ splitting completely in N; 128 and 1015 distinct vectors). *Upper bound, with the extra relation proved symbolically.* The product of the roots of g(Z) = Z³ − 30Z² − 24Z − 1 is z₁z₂z₃ = +1 (not −1), so a₁a₂a₃ = ±1, and ∏(1 + z\_i) = −g(−1) = 8, so C₁C₂C₃ = 8. From −3(1 − a⁴) = s(a)² one gets A\_iB\_i = 1 − a\_i² = −s(a\_i)²/(3C\_i). Hence for a labeling with a₁a₂a₃ = +1, C₁D₁A₂B₂D₂A₃B₃D₃ = C₁·(8a₁a₂a₃)·s(a₂)²s(a₃)²/(9C₂C₃) = 64s(a₂)²s(a₃)²/(9(C₂C₃)²) = (8s(a₂)s(a₃)/(3C₂C₃))², an explicit square in N, which is the relation reported by the dossier. The label-invariant form is ∏\_i C\_iD\_i = 64a₁a₂a₃ = ±64, that is \[C₁C₂C₃D₁D₂D₃\] = \[a₁a₂a₃\]; for a labeling with a₁a₂a₃ = −1 one replaces one D\_i by E\_i. With the four binary relations (the three triple sums A\_iB\_iC\_i and the three pair sums D\_iE\_i are each equal) the formal rank 15 drops to 11 and the extra relation gives 10. *Correction to the dossier's section 6.* The local sign at a root r of f modulo p is w\_p = −(g(r)/p) with g(r) = 2r(1 − r²), and since (1 − r²)(1 + r²) ≡ −3 modulo squares, (g(r)/p) = (−3/p)(2r/p)((1 + r²)/p). The factor ((1 + r²)/p) cannot be dropped in general: (g(r)/p) = (−3/p)(2r/p) at only 2956 of the 5938 roots of f modulo p for p < 60000 (0.498), while the complete formula holds at all 5938. It may be dropped exactly when 1 + r² is a square modulo p, which is the case for every root met by the orbit nP (y² = (1 − t)(1 + t²) with 1 − t a square), so along nP one has w\_p(E\_{t\_n}) = −(−3/p)(2t\_n/p). As the dossier says, \[L\* : L\] = 8 describes a finite character extension and does not by itself show variation of the global root numbers along the orbit or infinitely many specializations of rank at least 2.

**F.7 The fifth relation, derived from the cubic (Proved; checked for all 48 labelings).** Let z\_i = a\_i² be the roots of g(Z) = Z³ − 30Z² − 24Z − 1, so e₁ = 30, e₂ = −24, e₃ = 1, and put ε = a₁a₂a₃. Then ε² = e₃ = 1, so ε = ±1, and ∏(1 + z\_i) = 1 + e₁ + e₂ + e₃ = 8, so C₁C₂C₃ = 8, while D₁D₂D₃ = 8ε. Hence the *direct relation* ∏\_i C\_iD\_i = 64ε, that is \[C₁C₂C₃D₁D₂D₃\] = \[ε\], is a square exactly when ε = +1; it comes from the coefficients of the cubic alone and does not use the identity s² = −3(1 − T⁴). In the quotient basis of the dossier it is the product of this relation with two of the four binary relations: from A\_iB\_iC\_i = −s(a\_i)²/3 one has A\_iB\_i = −s(a\_i)²/(3C\_i), so C₁D₁A₂B₂D₂A₃B₃D₃ = C₁D₁D₂D₃·s(a₂)²s(a₃)²/(9C₂C₃) = ε·(8s(a₂)s(a₃)/(3C₂C₃))², using C₁ = 8/(C₂C₃). Verified at 60 digits for all 48 labelings (24 with ε = +1 and 24 with ε = −1): the displayed product equals ε·ρ², ρ = 8s(a₂)s(a₃)/(3C₂C₃), and ∏C\_iD\_i = 64ε. Consequences. (a) The relation is not in the span of the four binary relations (it contains no E\_i), and it is invariant under Gal(N/ℚ) because ε ∈ ℚ: the 24 relabelings induced by the Galois group are exactly the labelings with ε = +1, and for a labeling with ε = −1 the same product is −□, equivalently one replaces one D\_i by E\_i = −D\_i. (b) The same identities give a consistency check: ∏(1 − z\_i) = 1 − e₁ + e₂ − e₃ = −54 = ∏(−s(a\_i)²/(3C\_i)) forces s(a₁)s(a₂)s(a₃) = ±108, and indeed s(a₁)s(a₂)s(a₃) = 108ε, because ∏\_i s(a\_i) = ε·Res(g, Z² − 29Z − 26)/27 = 2916ε/27.

**F.8 Other families of classes, and a third kind of relation: z + 2 is a square (Proved exactly; the searches are certified lower bounds).** *Pair classes.* For the twenty classes A\_i, B\_i, C\_i, \[−1\], a\_i, a\_i + a\_j, a\_i − a\_j (i < j) and \[∏(z\_i − z\_j)/81\] = \[±√69\], the symbol vectors at the 463 primes below 1.2·10⁵ that split completely in N (all 24 labelings with a₁a₂a₃ = 1) have rank 16, and exactly four relations occur: a₁a₂a₃ = 1 (the cubic's constant term), the two relations A\_iB\_iC\_i = \[−3\] (the s-identity), and the tautology ∏(a\_i ± a\_j) = ∏(z\_i − z\_j); so the six classes \[a\_i ± a\_j\] are free (six new dimensions). For the classes \[z\_i + z\_j\] and \[1 ± a\_ia\_j\] the only relations are consequences of a₁a₂a₃ = 1 and the s-identity (for instance 1 ± a₁a₂ = (1 ± a₃⁻¹) gives p₁₂ ≡ B₃a₃ and q₁₂ ≡ −A₃a₃), and the three classes \[z\_i + z\_j\] are free. *A relation of a third kind.* A search over all 117649 polynomials P(a) of degree ≤ 5 with coefficients in \[−3, 3\] for which the Kummer class of P(a) in ℚ(a)^×/ℚ(a)^{×2} is a rational class (tested at 542 degree-one primes below 4000) finds, apart from trivial squares, only the multiples of 1 − a⁴ (class \[−3\]) and of a² + 2 (class \[1\]). The second is a new identity: with z = a² a root of g(Z) = Z³ − 30Z² − 24Z − 1,

z + 2 = ν², ν = (−z² + 32z + 14)/9 = 6 − 9/(z + 2),

exactly in ℚ(z) (the difference vanishes modulo g, and N(z + 2) = −g(−2) = 81). Equivalently g(Y − 2) = Y³ − 36Y² + 108Y − 81 = Y³ − (6Y − 9)², so with Y = v² the number v = 6 − 9/Y is a root of q(v) = v³ − 6v² + 9, g(v² − 2) = −q(v)q(−v), and the cubic field of the sextic is ℚ(v) with disc q = 3⁵·23 (totally real, N(v − k) = −q(k)). Hence z\_i = v\_i² − 2 and f(t) = ∏\_i (t² + 2 − v\_i²); the substitution t = (2 − u²)/(2u) splits the degree-12 polynomial (2u)⁶f((2 − u²)/(2u)) into the two sextics u⁶ ∓ 12u⁵ + 6u⁴ ± 24u³ + 12u² ∓ 48u + 8 over ℚ, and N = ℚ(v₁, v₂, v₃, √(v\_i² − 2)). This relation is of neither earlier kind: it is not a consequence of the s-identity or of the cubic's constants, and its class is trivial (so the counts of F.2, F.5 and F.6 are unchanged). The shape Y³ = (6Y − 9)² is that of a cuspidal cubic, which is where the singular fibres E\_t of the family with Δ = 0 are expected to put it; the classes of the elements v\_i − k of the totally real cubic field (norms −q(k)) are the natural next candidates for further relations.

**F.9 The classes of v − k in the cubic field (Proved: five explicit squares; rank 10 certified).** Let v be a root of q(v) = v³ − 6v² + 9 and K₃ = ℚ(v) (totally real, real roots ≈ −1.124, 1.399, 5.725). For the fifteen classes \[−1\], \[2\], \[3\], \[7\], \[23\] and \[v − k\], k = −3, …, 6, in K₃^×/K₃^{×2}, the symbols at the 25906 degree-one primes of K₃ below 3·10⁵ have rank 10, so there are at most five relations, and there are exactly five, each an explicit square in ℚ(v) (the difference with the square vanishes modulo q):

−(v − 6) = (v²/3 − 2v)², 23(v + 2) = (−v² + 2v + 8)², (v² − 9)(v − 5) = (v² − 5v)², (v² − 1)(v − 3)(v − 5) = (3 − v²/3)², v(v − 1) = (v²/3 − v)².

The first follows at once from v²(v − 6) = −9, and the norms explain the others: N(v + 2) = 23, N(v − 5) = 16, N(v) = −9, N(v − 1) = −4, N(v + 1) = −2, N(v − 3) = 18 (N(v − k) = −q(k)). In particular \[v\_i + 2\] = \[23\] is the same class for the three conjugates, and \[C\_i\] = \[v\_i² − 1\] = \[(v\_i − 3)(v\_i − 5)\], so the classes C\_i of the sieve are expressed through the v-classes. Among the old classes together with a\_i, v\_i and v\_i − k (46 classes, all labelings) the symbol vectors have rank 17 at the 1386 primes below 4·10⁵ that split completely in N; most of the many short relations found there are consequences of the five relations above and of their Galois conjugates, and the relations that hold in N but not in K₃ (for instance \[v\_i − 5\] = 1, where v − 5 is negative at two real embeddings) come from the non-real elements of N and have not been derived here.

**F.10 The five relations from ideal factorizations, and a reading of the Kummer ranks 7 and 10 (Proved, except where marked).** The field K₃ = ℚ(v), q(v) = v³ − 6v² + 9, has discriminant 621 = 3³·23 (ℤ\[v\] has index 3). Modulo 2, q = (v + 1)(v² + v + 1); modulo 3, q ≡ v³ (and 3 is totally ramified); modulo 7, q = (v − 2)(v² + 3v − 1); modulo 23, q = (v + 2)(v − 4)². So 2 = 𝔭₂𝔭₂′ (norms 2 and 4), 3 = 𝔭₃³, 7 = 𝔭₇𝔭₄₉, 23 = 𝔮𝔯² with 𝔮 = (23, v + 2) and 𝔯 = (23, v − 4) ramified. From N(v − k) = −q(k) the principal ideals are (v + 1) = 𝔭₂, (v − 1) = 𝔭₂², (v − 5) = 𝔭₂⁴, (v − 3) = 𝔭₂𝔭₃², (v + 3) = 𝔭₂³𝔭₃², (v) = (v − 6) = 𝔭₃², (v + 2) = 𝔮, (v − 4) = 𝔯, (v − 2) = 𝔭₇. The element v²/3 is integral (characteristic polynomial x³ − 12x² + 12x − 3, norm 3) and generates 𝔭₃, and 2/(v + 1) is integral of norm −4 and generates 𝔭₂′. By Minkowski's bound (6/27)√621 ≈ 5.5 only the primes of norm 2, 3, 4 matter (5 is inert), all principal, so the class number is 1. *Derivation of the relations.* The map from the 15 classes \[−1\], \[2\], \[3\], \[7\], \[23\], \[v − k\] to the parities of ideal exponents at 𝔭₂, 𝔭₂′, 𝔭₃, 𝔮, 𝔯, 𝔭₇, 𝔭₄₉ has rank 7 (the images of \[v + 1\], \[2\], \[3\], \[23\], \[v − 4\], \[v − 2\], \[7\] are independent). Its kernel has dimension 8 and is spanned by the classes with square ideal: \[−1\], \[v\], \[v − 1\], \[v − 5\], \[v − 6\], \[(v − 3)(v + 1)\], \[(v + 3)(v + 1)\], \[23(v + 2)\]. Since the class number is 1 these are classes of units, and the unit group of a totally real cubic field modulo squares has dimension 3 (units of rank 2 and −1), so at least 8 − 3 = 5 relations hold; five are found, hence the rank is exactly 7 + 3 = 10, and the five explicit squares of F.9 are the unit relations. Units occurring: (v − 1)/(v + 1)², 9/v³ = v/(v²/3)², (v − 5)/(v + 1)⁴ and z = a² = v² − 2 (norm 1: z is itself a unit, and 1 − z = 3 − v² has norm −54). *Relations involving z.* Adding \[z\] and \[1 − z\] to the fifteen classes does not raise the rank (still 10, 25906 primes), and gives the further relations \[v − 5\]\[z\] = 1, \[(v + 3)(v − 3)\]\[z\] = 1, \[(v² − 1)(v − 3)\]\[z\] = 1 and \[3\]\[v − 3\]\[v − 6\]\[3 − v²\] = 1. In N one has \[z\] = \[a²\] = 1, so \[v\_i − 5\] = 1, \[(v\_i + 3)(v\_i − 3)\] = 1 and \[C\_i\] = \[v\_i − 3\] hold there, which explains the relation \[v − 5\] = 1 that held in N but not in K₃ (F.9); with the s-identity A\_iB\_iC\_i = \[−3\] one also gets \[A\_iB\_i\] = \[−3\]\[v\_i − 3\]. *Reading the ranks 7 and 10.* The seven dimensions of the old classes decompose as 3 (the classes \[C\_i\] = \[v\_i − 3\], one for each conjugate of v) + 1 (the class \[−3\] = A\_iB\_iC\_i) + 3 (the quotients \[A\_i/B\_i\] = \[(1 − a\_i)/(1 + a\_i)\], which are not elements of ℚ(v)), and the extension of F.5 adds 1 (\[−1\]) + 2 (\[a₁\], \[a₂\], with a₁a₂a₃ = 1) for the total of 10. What this reading does not yet give is a proof of the independence of these classes by valuations (the lower bounds remain the symbol-vector certificates), in particular for the three quotients \[A\_i/B\_i\].

### F.B Relations to the literature and the search for a rational multisection (F.11–F.15)

**F.11 Relation to Verzobio's primitive-divisor theorems (Observed; framework only).** M. Verzobio, *Primitive divisors of sequences associated to elliptic curves*, J. Number Theory 209 (2020) 378–390, studies the denominators D\_n of x(nP + Q) for P of infinite order. Theorem 1.3: if Q is a torsion point of prime order, D\_n has a primitive divisor for all n ≥ C (ineffective, through Siegel's theorem); Theorem 1.5: for E over ℚ, P of infinite order and Q ∈ E(ℚ), the number of primes p ≤ x with Q ∈ ⟨P⟩ modulo p is ≫ √(log x); the link to the elliptic Artin (Lang–Trotter) conjecture is that Q ∈ ⟨P⟩ modulo p exactly when p divides some D\_n. *Application to J₂.* The translation by T = (0, 0) is x ↦ 2/x, so x(nP + T) = 2/x(nP) = 2D\_n²/m\_n² (checked for 1 ≤ n ≤ 90), and the denominator sequence of {nP + T} is, up to the factor 2, m\_n², the squared numerator root of x(nP) = m\_n²/D\_n². Theorem 1.3 with Q = T (prime order 2) therefore gives a primitive prime divisor of m\_n for all large n; computationally every m\_n with 3 ≤ n ≤ 90 has a prime dividing no earlier m\_k (the only exception is n = 2, where m₂ = 1). The same argument as in the paper gives the rank-of-apparition structure p | m\_n ⟹ r\_p | 2n and r\_p ∤ n (r\_p the order of P modulo p; no violation among the 40 prime–index pairs with p ≤ 10⁵ and n ≤ 25), so {m\_n} behaves like an elliptic divisibility sequence. *Limits.* The points R\_i over the roots of f that govern the primes of F\_n are non-torsion points over a field of degree 12, so neither Theorem 1.3 (torsion Q) nor Theorem 1.5 (Q ∈ E(ℚ)) applies to F\_n; the step rnP ≡ O in the proof uses that Q is torsion; the condition R\_i ∈ ⟨P⟩ modulo p of F.4 is exactly the set N\_{P,Q}(x), for which only a √(log x) lower bound is known unconditionally; and primitive divisors give new primes, not the parity of the number of prime factors or of their exponents, so they do not bear on Problem H.

**F.12 Relation to Helfgott's thesis (Conditional framework; read from the abstract and Chapter 1 of the thesis only; the equivalence stated in bold below is withdrawn in F.24).** H. A. Helfgott, *Root numbers and the parity problem*, Princeton Ph.D. thesis (2003), arXiv:math/0305435, studies the average of W(𝓔(t)) over t ∈ ℤ or t ∈ ℚ (lattice points (x, y) in sectors) for a one-parameter family 𝓔 over ℚ(t). Let M\_𝓔(x, y) be the product of the homogenized polynomials of the places of multiplicative reduction and B′\_𝓔 that of the quite bad places. Theorem 1.7.3 shows that if M\_𝓔 is not constant then W averages to zero over ℚ, conditionally on the square-free sieve for B′\_𝓔 (conjecture 𝔄₂) and on Chowla's conjecture 𝔅₂ for M\_𝓔 (the average of λ(M\_𝓔(x, y)) over ℤ² is zero); Propositions 1.7.9 and 1.7.10 show that, given 𝔄₂, the two statements are equivalent. Unconditional cases need deg\_irr(B′) ≤ 6 and deg M\_𝓔 ≤ 3 (Theorem 1.3′), and 𝔅₂ is proved for homogeneous forms of degree at most 3 (the square-free sieve 𝔄₂ is known for deg\_irr ≤ 6 by Greaves). *Our family.* The six I₁ fibres form one irreducible place, so M\_𝓔 = F(x, y) is the irreducible binary sextic and j is non-constant; B′ has irreducible factors of degree at most 6, so 𝔄₂ holds by Greaves, and Theorem 1.3′ does not apply because deg M\_𝓔 = 6 > 3. Hence, if I read the hypotheses correctly, **the average of W(E\_t) over t = u/v in a box is zero if and only if Chowla's conjecture 𝔅₂ holds for F(u, v)**, and the family is non-degenerate (W takes both signs, as the formula and the data show). This is the same parity barrier as in Problem H, now in its thick form. *Agreement with our decomposition.* Helfgott's §2.1 writes W as a pliable function times λ(M\_𝓔(x, y)) times a correction for non-squarefree values handled by the square-free sieve, and his Lemma 2.3.27 uses the Hilbert-symbol reciprocity ∏(a/𝔭)^{v\_𝔭(b)} = (pliable function)·∏(b/𝔭)^{v\_𝔭(a)}; our reciprocity reduction and the periodic factor ε\_n of the Theorem above are the analogue along the orbit, and the factor ∏ w\_p over primes of even exponent is his square-free correction. *Limits.* The thesis averages over thick sets of parameters; the orbit t\_n = 1 − x(nP) is a set of exponentially growing height to which none of its theorems applies, and nothing there gives infinitely many n with W(E\_{t\_n}) = +1. *A conditional route it suggests.* If the K3 surface S contained a curve R ≅ ℙ¹ over ℚ that is a multisection of the t-fibration, the base change by R → ℙ¹\_t would give a family over ℚ(s) with a tautological section, hence rank ≥ 1 for all but finitely many s (specialization), and Helfgott's theorem would give infinitely many s with W = +1 conditionally on 𝔅₂ for the pulled-back discriminant (a form of degree 6d) and 𝔄₂ if its degree exceeds 6, hence rank ≥ 2 if Sha\[2^∞\] is finite. No such rational curve has been found: the searches of §8 found only genus-1 and higher covers, and every irreducible factor of the pulled-back discriminant has degree divisible by 6, so an unconditional version is excluded.

**F.13 The search for a rational multisection: the conic base change and lattice constraints (negative results; one finite target).** *The conic.* The map t = (s² − 1)/(2s) is the double cover of the t-line by the conic w² = 1 + t² (the Pythagorean parametrization), and rank E(ℚ(s)) = rank E(ℚ(t)) + rank E^{(1+t²)}(ℚ(t)). The twist is y² = x³ + 4(1 − t⁴)x − 16t(1 + t²)², with discriminant proportional to (t² + 1)³f(t) of degree 12: fibres 2·III + 6·I₁, a rational elliptic surface whose Mordell–Weil group has rank 8 − 2 = 6 over ℚ̄(t). Counting points on the surface modulo p (the counts satisfy N ≡ 1 + p² mod p at every prime tested) gives the Frobenius trace on the rank-6 lattice, whose average over the 376 primes 5 ≤ p < 2600 is −0.016, so the rank over ℚ(t) is 0. The same traces give the multiplicity of every quadratic character χ\_c in the Galois representation on the lattice, that is the rank of E^{c(1+t²)} over ℚ(t) (the rank of E over the cover w² = c(1 + t²)): for all squarefree c with |c| ≤ 120 the multiplicity is within noise of 0 (the largest is 0.12). So no double cover of the t-line branched at t = ±i gives positive rank. *Lattice constraints.* Since the generic Mordell–Weil group is trivial, NS(S) = U ⊕ E₇ ⊕ E₇, and Galois (i ↦ −i) swaps the two E₇ summands, so a ℚ-rational curve R has class D = dO + bF − (λ, λ) with d = D·F the degree over the t-line, λ a dominant weight of E₇ in the root lattice (n₂ + n₅ + n₇ even) taken in both summands, D² = 2δ − 2 with δ the number of nodes, d·b = d² + δ − 1 + |λ|². The necessary conditions for an irreducible curve are D·O = b − 2d ≥ 0, D·Θ\_j = n\_j ≥ 0 and D·Θ₀ = d − Σm\_jn\_j ≥ 0 at each III\* fibre (multiplicities m\_j = 2, 2, 3, 4, 3, 2, 1). Enumerating: there is **no smooth rational ℚ-curve (δ = 0) of degree d ≤ 4 over the t-line**; the smallest is d = 5, with one class (λ = ω₂ + 3ω₇, b = 10, D·O = 0), then d = 9 and d = 11. A bisection (d = 2) must be nodal with D² = 0, so it is a member of a genus-1 pencil: there are two classes, the x-fibration (λ = ω₆, b = 4, D·O = 0) and λ = 2ω₇ (b = 5, D·O = 1); the singular members of the x-fibration {x = c} are at the roots of (c²)³ − 16c² − 64 = 0, which has no rational root, so no rational bisection of that pencil exists over ℚ (as found in §8). The smallest class of rational trisections is d = 3, δ = 2, λ = ω₂ + ω₇, b = 6, D² = 2, D·O = D·Θ₀ = 0: the net |D| gives a double plane S → ℙ², its rational members are the preimages of the bitangents of the branch sextic, and a ℚ-rational bitangent would give a rational base change of degree 3 with a section, disjoint from O. Constructing this net explicitly and testing for rational bitangents is a finite computation that has not been done. *Consequence.* The conditional route of F.12 requires such a curve; none exists in degree ≤ 2 over ℚ, and in degree 3 only this nodal class remains.

**F.14 The rational trisection class: the net, the double plane and its bitangents (Computed; negative result).** *The net |D|.* Let D = 3O + 6F − Σ\_{t = ±i}Σ\_k c\_kΘ\_k with c = C⁻¹(e₂ + e₇) = (3, 5, 6, 9, 7, 5, 3) (Bourbaki labels), the class with λ = ω₂ + ω₇ of F.13. In the integral model Y² = X³ + 4ac³X − 8bc⁵ (a = 1 − t², b = 2t, c = 1 + t²) the singular point of the III\* fibre at t = ±i is the E₇ point y² = x³ + (unit)τ³x + O(τ⁵) with τ = t ∓ i. Putting it in normal form by ξ = X − ρ(τ), where ρ is the root of X³ + a₄X + a₆ of valuation 2 (so that Y² = ξ(ξ² + 3ρξ + A + 3ρ²) with no constant term), the seven exceptional curves are the crepant monomial valuations (w\_τ, w\_ξ, w\_Y) = (2,2,3), (2,3,5), (3,4,6), (4,6,9), (3,5,7), (2,4,5), (1,3,3) for Θ₁, …, Θ₇ (found as the seven solutions of w\_τ + w\_ξ + w\_Y = ν(f) + 1 with at least two monomials of minimal weight, and matched to the Dynkin diagram through midpoints: (3,4,6) = ½((2,2,3) + (4,6,9)), (3,5,7) = ½((4,6,9) + (2,4,5)), (2,4,5) = ½((3,5,7) + (1,3,3))). For f = p₀ + p₂X + p₃Y with deg p₀ ≤ 6, deg p₂ ≤ 2, p₃ constant (dimension 11), the conditions ν\_k(f) ≥ c\_k are ord(p₀ + p₂ρ) ≥ 3, ord(p₂) ≥ 1 and none on p₃, that is 8 real linear conditions, leaving exactly dimension 3 as Riemann–Roch requires (D² = 2, h⁰ = 3). The solutions are f = λc³ + κcX + μY = c³(λ + κx + μy), so |D| is the pullback of the lines λ + κx + μy = 0 of the (x, y)-plane. *The double plane.* S → ℙ²\_{(x,y)}, (t, x, y) ↦ (x, y), has degree 2: eliminating B = (x³ + 4Ax − y²)/8 from A² + B² = 1 gives a quadratic in A with discriminant −F/16 for the **branch sextic F = (y² − x³)² − 16x² − 64**. F is irreducible over ℚ, its affine part is smooth (the Gröbner basis of (F, F\_x, F\_y) is {1}), its only singular point is (0 : 1 : 0) at infinity, and with x = r − 1/r its normalization is the hyperelliptic curve Y² = r(r⁶ + r⁴ + 7r² − 1) of genus 3. A line gives a rational member of |D| exactly when it is a bitangent (two nodes on the preimage). *Bitangents.* For y = mx + q the polynomial F(x, mx + q) is monic of degree 6; imposing F(x, mx + q) = (x² + ax + b)²(x² + cx + d) gives six equations in (a, b, c, d, m, q); eliminating c and d leaves four equations whose grevlex Gröbner basis has 35 elements and is zero-dimensional; converting to lex order (FGLM) the eliminant in q has degree 60 and is **irreducible over ℚ**. Hence there is no bitangent with rational slope and intercept; vertical lines x = c cannot be bitangent, since F(c, y) = (y² − c³ − κ)(y² − c³ + κ) with κ = 4√(c² + 4) ≠ 0 would need both factors to have a double root at y = 0. So **no ℚ-rational rational trisection of this class exists**, and with F.13 there is no ℚ-rational rational multisection of degree ≤ 3 (smooth ones are excluded up to degree 4 by the enumeration, and nodal ones of degree 2 and 3 by the pencil and this bitangent computation). *The degree-5 class.* The unique candidate smooth class of degree 5 (λ = ω₂ + 3ω₇ at both fibres, b = 10) is automatically Galois-invariant, so if irreducible it would be a ℚ-conic; but over ℚ̄ there are numerically possible (−2)-classes of degree 2, 2O + 4F − (2ω₇ at one fibre, ω₆ at the other) and its conjugate, with intersection number −1 with it, and rational bisections over ℚ̄ exist (the twist E^{(1+t²)} has Mordell–Weil rank 6 over ℚ̄(t)); if these classes are realised by curves the degree-5 class is reducible. This is settled in F.15 (the degree-5 class is not represented by an irreducible curve).

**F.15 The degree-5 class and all smooth invariant classes up to degree 21 are not curves (Proved; the argument for the degree-5 class is due to the reviewer, the extension is computed).** *The class R.* Let R = 2O + 4F − 2ω₇ (at the fibre t = i) − ω₆ (at the fibre t = −i), and R̄ its Galois conjugate. Both 2ω₇ and ω₆ lie in the root lattice of E₇ (n₂ + n₅ + n₇ is even), so R is an integral class generated by O, F and fibre components; |2ω₇|² = 6, |ω₆|² = 4, (2O + 4F)² = 8, hence R² = 8 − 6 − 4 = −2 and R·F = 2. By Riemann–Roch on a K3 surface (χ = 2 + R²/2 = 1) R or −R is effective, and −R is not because F is nef and (−R)·F = −2; so R is effective (it need not be nef or irreducible). *The degree-5 class.* D₅ = 5O + 10F − (ω₂ + 3ω₇) at each fibre has D₅² = −2 and D₅·R = −20 + 20 + 20 − \[(ω₂ + 3ω₇, 2ω₇) + (ω₂ + 3ω₇, ω₆)\] = 20 − (12 + 9) = −1, using (ω₂, ω₇) = 3/2, (ω₇, ω₇) = 3/2, (ω₂, ω₆) = 3, (ω₇, ω₆) = 2. If an irreducible curve C had class D₅, then C·R < 0 would force C to be a component of every effective divisor of the class R; but C·F = 5 > 2 = R·F and every component has non-negative degree over the t-line, a contradiction. So **no irreducible curve has the class D₅**. *Extension.* The same argument shows that an irreducible curve C of degree d > 2 satisfies C·R′ ≥ 0 for every effective class R′ of degree < d, in particular for every integral class with R′² = −2 and 0 < R′·F < d (effective by Riemann–Roch, whatever its other intersections). For all smooth ℚ-invariant candidate classes (δ = 0) of odd degree d ≤ 21 (one class for d = 5, 9, 11; two for d = 13; one for d = 15; four for d = 17; three for d = 19; four for d = 21) a search over the integral (−2)-classes of lower degree finds in every case one of degree 2 with D·R′ between −1 and −5 (the values −1, −2, −2, −2 and −3, −3, between −4 and −2, between −4 and −3, between −5 and −3), so none is represented by an irreducible curve: **there is no smooth rational ℚ-curve of degree ≤ 21 over the t-line**. For a nodal class (D² = 2δ − 2 ≥ 0) the same inequality gives a lower bound on the number of nodes: with R = 2O + 4F − 2ω₇ − ω₆, D·R = 2b − 2(λ, ω₇) − (λ, ω₆) ≥ 0 where b = (d² + δ − 1 + |λ|²)/d, so a rational multisection of degree d needs δ large enough. The case d = 3 is the class of F.14 (δ = 2), for which no ℚ-curve exists.

### F.C The parity problem: what is proved and what is not (F.16–F.29)

**F.16 Where the Liouville values come from, and the weaker variants (Computed; one provable variant).** *p-adic structure.* For a prime p dividing F\_n and n in a class a modulo r\_p, nP − R lies in the formal group over ℤ\_p, and the logarithm gives v\_p(F\_n) = 1 + v\_p((n − N\_p)/r\_p) for a p-adic integer N\_p attached to the class: the set of n with p^e | F\_n is one class modulo r\_pp^{e−1} for each class modulo r\_p. This is the mechanism behind F.15's one lift per class and was checked at the levels e = 1, 2, 3 for p = 23 (2 classes modulo 10, 46 residues modulo 10·23^e at every level) and e = 1, 2 for p = 83 (4 classes modulo 39, 332 residues modulo 39·83^e). So each prime contributes a completely explicit p-adic 'centre', and λ(F\_n) = ∏\_p(−1)^{v\_p(F\_n)} is a product of such local factors. *The rough part.* The primes below 3·10⁴ that divide some F\_n (358 of them) explain only a negligible part of F\_n: for n = 10, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100, 120 the digits explained are 6.1, 0.0, 9.3, 0.0, 3.3, 10.9, 4.2 out of 113, 450, 1802, 4055, 7210, 11266, 16223 digits. The part of F\_n supported on primes p with r\_p > n occurs for at most one class and carries more than 99.9% of log F\_n for n ≥ 40; its parity is untouched by any sieve (this is the parity problem), and heuristically ω(F\_n) is Poisson with mean about log log F\_n ≈ 2 log n, so λ(F\_n) equidistributes while the number of n with F\_n having at most k prime factors is finite. *Weaker variants.* (i) *Quadratic twists (provable).* For q ∤ N\_E prime, W(E^{(q)}) = W(E)χ\_q(−N\_E); since E\_{t\_n} has a non-torsion point, s₂(E\_{t\_n}/ℚ) ≥ 1, and if W(E^{(q)}) = −1 then s₂(E^{(q)}/ℚ) is odd, hence ≥ 1, and the 2^∞-Selmer corank over ℚ(√q) is s₂(E/ℚ) + s₂(E^{(q)}/ℚ) ≥ 2. Both values of χ\_q(−N\_E) occur for infinitely many q, so for every n there are infinitely many quadratic fields over which E\_{t\_n} has Selmer corank ≥ 2 (rank ≥ 2 if Sha\[2^∞\] is finite); this does not give rank ≥ 2 over ℚ. (ii) The pair version (W(E\_{t\_n}) = +1 or W(E\_{t\_n′}) = +1 infinitely often) is slightly weaker, since its negation needs the Liouville values of both sequences locked to periodic patterns, but it is not accessible either. (iii) Statements about averages, logarithmic densities or subsequences in progressions are equivalent in difficulty because they all contain the parity of the rough part.

**F.17 Test of the elliptic-net idea: no flow of information between different n (Computed; negative).** The values F\_n are norms of values at nP of the Miller-type function f\_R(Q) = x(Q) − x(R) = −σ(Q + R)σ(Q − R)/(σ(Q)²σ(R)²), that is slices of a rank-2 elliptic net W(n, m) for the points P and R. The net relation W(p+q+s)W(p−q)W(r+s)W(r) + W(q+r+s)W(q−r)W(p+s)W(p) + W(r+p+s)W(r−p)W(q+s)W(q) = 0 with p, q, r on the P-axis and s = (0, 1) expresses a relation among the terms W(·, 1) and the EDS terms ψ\_n of P; it is a sum of three products, not a single product, so it gives no multiplicative relation between the values λ(F\_n) of the Liouville function at different n (λ is not additive). The only way a relation between λ(F\_n) and λ(F\_m) could arise is therefore through shared primes. Measured for 2 ≤ m ≤ 64, the part of F\_n supported on primes that also divide some other F\_m has 0.0, 3.9, 3.0, 1.8, 0.0, 2.7, 4.8, 0.0 digits for n = 4, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48, 64, out of 17.8, 71.9, 162.9, 287.2, 647.8, 1154.1, 2595.2, 4613.9 digits, that is at most 5.4% for n = 8 and at most 0.3% for n ≥ 32; these shared primes are the small primes of the explicit classes of F.15–F.16. So the prime sets of different F\_n are essentially disjoint, λ(F\_n) and λ(F\_m) are products over essentially disjoint sets of primes, and no information on the parity of the rough part passes from one index to another. The complete factorizations available (n = 2, …, 9) give λ(F\_n) = +1, +1, +1, −1, +1, +1, +1, −1 with 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5 prime factors, with no pattern in n modulo 6 (+1 at n = 3 and −1 at n = 9), but eight values prove nothing.

**F.18 The squarefree part of F\_n tends to infinity (Proved, by Faltings' theorem; ineffective).** Write x(nP) = A\_n/B\_n² in lowest terms and C\_n = B\_n¹²h(A\_n/B\_n²) with h(x) = f(1 − x) = x⁶ − 6x⁵ − 15x⁴ + 100x³ − 189x² + 162x − 54; since t\_n = (B\_n² − A\_n)/B\_n², C\_n = F(U\_n, V\_n) = F\_n. The function h(x(Q)) on J₂ has the twelve distinct simple zeros of F.16 and a pole of order 12 (even) at O, so for any fixed non-zero d ∈ ℚ the double cover X\_d : w² = h(x)/d of J₂ is geometrically irreducible, branched at exactly twelve points, and by Riemann–Hurwitz 2g − 2 = 2(2·1 − 2) + 12 gives g = 7. *Proposition.* For every fixed squarefree integer d (of either sign) there are only finitely many n with C\_n = d·u², u ∈ ℤ. *Proof.* If C\_n = du² then h(x(nP))/d = (u/B\_n⁶)², so nP lifts to a point of X\_d(ℚ); the points nP are pairwise distinct because P has infinite order, so infinitely many such n would give infinitely many rational points on a curve of genus 7, contradicting Faltings' theorem. □ *Consequences.* The set of squarefree integers supported on a finite set of primes S is finite (at most 2^{|S|+1} signed classes), so for every finite S all sufficiently large n have a prime p ∉ S dividing F\_n to an odd power; equivalently sqf(|F\_n|) → ∞, and in particular F\_n is a perfect square for only finitely many n. The constants are not effective. *What it does not give.* The squarefree part may tend to infinity through a single large prime, so the proposition does not give ω(sqf(|F\_n|)) → ∞, let alone both parities: λ(|F\_n|) = (−1)^{ω(sqf(|F\_n|))} may still be constant for all large n. The same argument applies to the translate sequence nP + T and to any sequence nP + Q with Q ∈ J₂(ℚ). (The relevant statement on ordinary signs of elliptic divisibility sequences is that of Silverman and Stephens, not about Liouville signs.)

**F.19 No multiplication or translation identity for φ = h(x(Q)) (Proved).** Let D be the reduced divisor of the twelve zeros of φ on J₂, so div φ = D − 12(O). *Lemma.* Let α : J₂ → J₂ be an étale morphism of degree δ (an isogeny followed by a translation) and suppose φ∘α = cφψ² with c ∈ ℚ̄^× and ψ ∈ ℚ̄(J₂)^×. Then α^{−1}(D) = D as sets, so δ = 1. *Proof.* div(φ∘α) = α^*D − 12α^*(O) and the pole divisor has even coefficients, so the identity forces α^\*D − D ≡ 0 (mod 2) as divisors; α^\*D is reduced (α étale) with 12δ points and D is reduced, so every point of one set lying outside the other has odd coefficient, hence the two sets coincide, and 12δ = 12. □ *Proposition.* For every integer m with |m| ≥ 2 there is no identity φ(\[m\]Q) = cφ(Q)ψ(Q)² over ℚ̄, a fortiori over ℚ: the ratio φ(\[m\]Q)/φ(Q) is not in ℚ̄^×ℚ̄(J₂)^{×2} (here δ = m² ≥ 4, and \[m\]^\*D has 12m² points). For m = 2 this agrees with gcd(h, H₂) = 1 for the degree-48 divisor \[2\]^\*D (Appendix D). *Translations.* By the Lemma an identity φ(Q + R) = cφ(Q)ψ² requires R + D = D, so R would permute the twelve points; for the three 2-torsion points T\_j = (e\_j, 0), e\_j ∈ {0, 1 + i, 1 − i}, the numerator of h(x(Q + T\_j)) has gcd of degree 0 with h over ℚ(i), so the supports are disjoint and no identity holds (for T = (0, 0) this is gcd(F, G) = 1 with G(x) = x⁶F(2/x), Appendix E.1); torsion translations of other orders were not checked. *Scope.* This excludes identities between values of φ at Q and at α(Q) that come from rational functions on J₂. It says nothing about identities that hold only at the points nP, and it does not contradict the point-specific identity c(M(α)) = −s(α)² of Appendix D, which concerns the values of a different function (the character) at the twelve zeros of φ, not φ itself.

**F.20 The valuations at 2 and 3 of the values of the sextic along nP and nP + T (Proved; checked exactly for 1 ≤ n ≤ 200).** Write x(nP) = a\_n/d\_n in lowest terms (a\_n = m\_n², d\_n = D\_n² are squares, F.18) and F\_n = d\_n⁶h(a\_n/d\_n), G\_n = d\_n⁶h\_T(a\_n/d\_n) with h\_T(X) = X⁶h(2/X) = −54X⁶ + 324X⁵ − 756X⁴ + 800X³ − 240X² − 192X + 64. Since x(nP + T) = 2/x(nP) = 2d\_n/a\_n with a\_n a square, G\_n = a\_n⁶h(2d\_n/a\_n) is the value C′\_n of F.18 for the sequence nP + T. *Theorem.* For every n ≥ 1: v₂(F\_n) = v₃(F\_n) = 0, v₂(G\_n) = 1, and v₃(G\_n) = 4 if n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9) and 3 otherwise. *Proof.* (a) At 2 the curve reduces to y² = x³ and P = (1, 1) to a nonsingular point of order 2, so a\_n is odd for every n (d\_n is odd for n odd and even for n even); F ≡ a⁶ + a⁴d² + a²d⁴ ≡ 1 (mod 2), and G ≡ −54a⁶ ≡ 2 (mod 4) because all other coefficients of h\_T are divisible by 4. (b) The discriminant −256 gives good reduction at 3 and P has order 3 modulo 3 (3P = (49/9, −287/27)). If 3 | n then 9 | d\_n and 3 ∤ a\_n, so F ≡ a⁶ ≡ 1 (mod 3), and in G the term −54a⁶ has valuation 3 while every other term has valuation at least 6; if 3 ∤ n then x(nP) ≡ 1 (mod 3) and h(1) = −1 ≢ 0, so 3 ∤ F\_n. (c) For 3 ∤ n write x(nP) = 1 + 3z; the identity h\_T(1 + 3z) = −54(729z⁶ − 81z⁴ + 32z³ − 9z² + 1) = −54Q(z) holds, Q(z) ≡ 2z + 1 (mod 3) vanishes mod 3 only for z ≡ 1 (mod 3), and for z = 1 + 3w, Q ≡ 6 (mod 9), so v₃(Q) = 1 exactly when x(nP) ≡ 4 (mod 9) and 0 otherwise; d\_n is a 3-adic unit. (d) The formal parameter of 3P is −x/y = 147/287 of valuation 1, so 9P = 3 lies in the second kernel of reduction, 9P ≡ O (mod 9), and nP mod 9 depends only on n mod 9. From x(P) = 1, x(2P) = 1/4 ≡ 7, x(4P) = 961/400 ≡ 4 and x(−Q) = x(Q) one gets x(nP) mod 9 = ∞, 1, 7, ∞, 4, 4, ∞, 7, 1 for n ≡ 0, …, 8, so x(nP) ≡ 4 (mod 9) exactly for n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9). □ *Consequences.* With λ\_{2,3}(N) = (−1)^{v₂(N)+v₃(N)}: λ\_{2,3}(F\_n) = +1 for all n and λ\_{2,3}(G\_n) = −1 if n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9), +1 otherwise (the same for F\_nG\_n). Hence λ(|F\_n|) is the Liouville value of the part prime to 6 of F\_n, and the full Liouville value of the translate's C-value differs from that of its part prime to 6 by the explicit period-9 sign −1 for n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9); the lemma gives the 3-adic structure behind the period 9 (period 18 with the 2-adic factor) of the finite factor of the root number of E\_{t′\_n} but does not by itself determine the local root numbers w₂, w₃, (2/c), and it says nothing about the primes p ≥ 5.

**F.21 The period-3 law along nP + T (Theorem; proof complete, with three finite local evaluations done by exact computation; checked directly for 2 ≤ n ≤ 200).** Let t′\_n = 1 − x(nP + T) = 1 − 2/x(nP) = U′\_n/V′\_n and ε′\_n = ε(t′\_n) with the exact definition of Appendix B. *Theorem.* For every n ≥ 2, ε′\_n = +1 if and only if 3 | n. *Proof.* (1) With x(nP) = m²/D², t′\_n = (m² − 2D²)/m², so V′ = m² is an odd square (a\_n = m² is odd by F.20), gcd(U′, V′) = 1 and the common factor of the Pythagorean triple of t′ is 2; moreover t′ < 1 and t′ ∉ {0, ±1}. (2) Because V′ is a square, the reciprocity formula of Appendix B applies verbatim: ε(t′) = f(t′)·∏\_{v ∈ {2,3,∞}} (2t(1−t²), f(t))\_v (−1, t)\_v (−6, 1−t²)\_v, where the primes p ≥ 5 dividing U′V′(V′² − U′²) cause no trouble since F ≡ −V⁶, U⁶, −54V⁶ modulo them; the 2- and 3-parts of the value G\_n (F.20) enter only through the symbols at 2 and 3. (3) Real place: the real factor is +1 for every t < 1, t ≠ 0 (checked on 2313 random points; by cases t < −1, −1 < t < 0, 0 < t < 1). (4) Hence ε′\_n = Ψ₂(t′\_n)Ψ₃(t′\_n) with Ψ₂ = −w₂·(2/c)·h₂ and Ψ₃ = w₃·h₃; each depends only on t′\_n as an element of ℚ₂ resp. ℚ₃, since w₂, w₃ are read from A = (1−t²)/(1+t²), B = 2t/(1+t²) modulo 16 resp. 9 (continuous in t, 1 + t² ≠ 0) and (2/c) = +1 here (c ≡ 1 mod 8: V′² ≡ 1 mod 16 and (1 + t′²)/2 ≡ 1 mod 8 for t′ ≡ ±1 mod 8). (5) Classes of t′\_n, from the valuation laws of Appendix B and F.20: 2-adically, n odd: v₂(t′ + 1) = 1 + v₂(n² − 1) ≥ 4; n ≡ 2 (mod 4): t′ ≡ −7 (mod 64) (x′ = 8D′²/m² with D′ odd); n ≡ 0 (mod 4): v₂(x′) = 1 + 2v₂(n) ≥ 5, so t′ ≡ 1 (mod 32). 3-adically, 3 | n: v₃(t′ − 1) = 2v₃(n) ≥ 2; 3 ∤ n: t′ ≡ 8, 2, 5 (mod 9) for n ≡ ±1, ±2, ±4 (mod 9) (from the table of F.20 via x′ = 2/x). (6) Local constancy: on each of these classes Ψ₂ resp. Ψ₃ is constant. Near the singular points t = ±1 the symbols containing 1 − t² = ε·(unit) cancel (the residue vanishes because −6f(±1) = 324 = 18² is a square) and f(t)/f(±1) is a square on the stated balls (relative perturbation ≡ 0 mod 8 resp. mod 3); on the classes t ≡ −7 (mod 64) and t ≡ 2, 5 (mod 9) all unit square classes of t, 1 − t², f(t) are fixed and w₂, w₃ are determined by t modulo 64 resp. 9. This was confirmed on 300 random rational points u/m² per class (all constant). (7) The constants, by exact evaluation at n = 2, 3, 4, 8: Ψ₂ = −1 on all three 2-adic classes; Ψ₃ = −1 for 3 | n, and Ψ₃ = +1 on the three classes t′ ≡ 8, 2, 5 (mod 9). Therefore ε′\_n = (−1)(−1) = +1 for 3 | n and (−1)(+1) = −1 for 3 ∤ n. □ *By-products (proved above, checked for n ≤ 200).* G\_n ≡ 10 (mod 16) for every n, so G\_n/2 ≡ 5 (mod 8) and the part N′\_n of |G\_n| prime to 6 satisfies (2/N′\_n) = −1 exactly when n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9). *Consequence.* The period-6 theorem for nP (Appendix B) and the period-3 theorem for nP + T are now both proved; the sign ε of the root number is completely determined along both orbits, and what remains of H is exactly the parity of the number of prime factors p ≥ 5 (with multiplicity) of F\_n resp. G\_n, i.e. the Chowla problem of F.12.

**F.22 The valuation laws v₂(D\_n) = v₂(n) and v₃(D\_n) = v₃(n) from the formal group (Theorem; proved; checked for 1 ≤ n ≤ 300; closes open problem 2).** Write x(nP) = m\_n²/D\_n² in lowest terms, D\_n > 0 (so V\_n = D\_n²). *Theorem.* For every n ≥ 1, v₂(D\_n) = v₂(n) and v₃(D\_n) = v₃(n). *Proof.* Let z = −x/y be the formal parameter and E\_r = {Q : v(z(Q)) ≥ r}. Three facts. (a) If v\_p(x(Q)) = −2r < 0 then v\_p(z(Q)) = r, because on y² = x³ − 2x² + 2x the term x³ has strictly smaller valuation than 2x² and 2x; so "depth r" and "v(x) = −2r" are the same statement, and v\_p(D) = r. (b) For a unit k ∈ ℤ\_p, k = kz + z²g(z) with g ∈ ℤ\_p\[\[z\]\], hence v(\[k\]z) = v(z) whenever v(z) ≥ 1. (c) Doubling: x(2Q) = (x² − 2)²/(4x(x² − 2x + 2)) = (x/4)·(1 − 2x⁻²)²/(1 − 2x⁻¹ + 2x⁻²); if v₂(x) = −2r with r ≥ 1 the last factor is a 2-adic unit, so v₂(x(2Q)) = v₂(x) − 2: doubling raises the 2-adic depth by exactly 1 (checked on 59 points). *p = 2.* Modulo 2 the curve is y² = x³ with singular point (0, 0); the nonsingular points are O and (1, 1), and P̄ = (1, 1) has order 2 since 2P = (1/4, −5/8) reduces to O. So for n odd, nP̄ = P̄ and x(nP) ∈ ℤ₂, D\_n is odd; for n even nP ∈ E₁, and 2P has v₂(x) = −2, i.e. depth 1 (z(2P) = 2/5). For n = 2^s k, s ≥ 1, k odd, nP = k: by (c) iterated s − 1 times the point 2^{s−1} has depth exactly s, and by (b) multiplication by the odd k keeps it. By (a), v₂(D\_n) = s = v₂(n). *p = 3.* The discriminant −256 gives good reduction at 3; 2P = (1/4, −5/8) is 3-integral and 3P = (49/9, −287/27) reduces to O, so P̄ has order exactly 3. If 3 ∤ n then nP̄ ≠ O and 3 ∤ D\_n. If 3 | n then 3P ∈ E₁ with z(3P) = 21/41 of valuation exactly 1. For p = 3 the condition v(z) > v(p)/(p − 1) = 1/2 holds on E₁, so v₃(z(\[3\]Q)) = 1 + v₃(z(Q)) there (Silverman, AEC, IV Prop. 6.4). Writing n = 3^s k with 3 ∤ k, nP = k has depth exactly 1 + (s − 1) = s, and (a) gives v₃(D\_n) = s = v₃(n). □ *Remark.* The same argument gives, for every prime p of good reduction at which P̄ has order r\_p (the rank of apparition) and p odd, v\_p(D\_n) = 0 if r\_p ∤ n and v\_p(D\_n) = v\_p(D\_{r\_p}) + v\_p(n/r\_p) if r\_p | n; this is the standard elliptic-divisibility-sequence law and explains why different F\_n share almost no primes (F.18). Together with Appendix B and F.20 it removes the last unproved input behind the period-6 and period-3 theorems.

**F.23 A reformulation that does not help (Remark).** For odd N > 0 one has N ≡ (−1)^{Ω₃(N)} (mod 4), where Ω₃ counts prime factors ≡ 3 (mod 4) with multiplicity, hence λ(N) = χ₄(N)·(−1)^{Ω₁(N)}, and along our sequences χ₄(|F\_n|) = (−1)^n·sgn(F\_n) (F\_n ≡ 1 (mod 8) for n even, 7 (mod 8) for n odd). This is an identity for every odd integer; it replaces the parity of Ω by the parity of Ω₁ with a known explicit sign, so the two problems are equivalent, and sgn(F\_n) is not periodic (nP equidistributes on the real component). It does not reduce the Chowla problem of F.12.

**F.24 Precise status of the remaining parity bit (Correction and Proposition).** *Correction of F.12.* The statement that H is "equivalent" to Chowla's conjecture for F(u, v) is withdrawn. Helfgott's theorem concerns the average of W(E\_t) over the full two-parameter family t = u/v; the orbit t\_n is a density-zero subset of any such family. Chowla for F(u, v) in two variables does not imply anything along the thin sequence (U\_n, V\_n), and H, which only asks for infinitely many n with W = +1, does not imply Chowla (it asks for no cancellation). What is true is the weaker statement of the barrier in §6 (the paragraph "A barrier for the sign"): the unconditional equidistribution methods do not apply to a degree-6 form, and a sign statement needs a parity-type input for values of F along the orbit. Neither implication with Chowla is established. *The bit.* Since v₂(F\_n) = v₃(F\_n) = 0 (F.20), the part of |F\_n| prime to 6 is |F\_n| itself, and the unknown is δ\_n = λ(|F\_n|) = (−1)^{ω(rad)}·(−1)^{Σ(e\_p − 1)} (the second factor accounts exactly for repeated primes). *Proposition.* Assuming the exact root-number formula W = f(t\_n)·(−1)^{ω(R)}·(ab/R) with R the radical of F″\_n, and with ε\_n = f(t\_n)·(ab/F″\_n), one has W(E\_{t\_n}) = ε\_n·δ\_n·κ\_n, where κ\_n = ∏\_{p : e\_p even} (−(ab/p)) (so κ\_n = 1 when every exponent is odd). Consequently, H fails if and only if W\_n = −1 for all n ≥ n₀, and for those n with F\_n squarefree this says δ\_n = −ε\_n: the Liouville function along the orbit would be eventually equal to the explicit period-6 pattern −ε\_n. *Remark on targets.* (i) Cancellation of δ\_n alone, or a bound |Σ\_{n≤N} δ\_n| ≤ (1 − c)N, does not suffice for H: the pattern δ\_n = −ε\_n has bounded partial sums (ε is periodic with ε\_n = +1 exactly for n ≡ 0, 1, 5 (mod 6), three plus signs and three minus signs per period, so its mean is 0) and still gives W ≡ −1. The correct target is a bound for the sums of W\_n = ε\_nδ\_nκ\_n, i.e. non-correlation of δ\_n with the periodic function ε\_n, for instance |Σ\_{n≤N} W\_n| ≤ (1 − c)N, which forces both signs of W with positive lower density. (ii) Squarefreeness of F\_n along a positive proportion of n is not known (F.18 gives sqf(|F\_n|) → ∞ and infinitely many non-squarefree F\_n), so Route A (restricting to squarefree indices) is itself open, and even on such indices the parity barrier remains.

**F.25 Universal coprimality of the sequences along nP and nP + T (Theorem; proved; verified independently by exact computation).** With x(nP) = A\_n/B\_n², F\_n = B\_n¹²h(A\_n/B\_n²) and G\_n = B\_n¹²h\_T(A\_n/B\_n²) (F.20; G\_n is the value of the sextic along nP + T), *Theorem.* gcd(|F\_n|, |G\_n|) = 1 for every n ≥ 1. *Proof.* F\_n = F\_h(A\_n, B\_n²) and G\_n = F\_h(2B\_n², A\_n) are values of two binary sextics at a coprime pair, so a common prime p divides their resultant, |Res(h, h\_T)| = 2¹⁷·7·103·519521² (recomputed). The prime 2 is excluded because F\_n is odd (F.20: v₂(F\_n) = 0). If p | B\_n then F\_n ≡ A\_n⁶ ≢ 0 (mod p) (h is monic), so p ∤ F\_n. Otherwise x(nP) reduces to a common root r ∈ 𝔽\_p of h and h\_T, and (r, ·) must be an 𝔽\_p-point of J lying in ⟨P̄⟩. p = 7: the only common root is r = 4, and r³ − 2r² + 2r is a non-square mod 7, so r is the x-coordinate of no 𝔽₇-point. p = 103: the only common root is r = 38, again the x-coordinate of no 𝔽\_{103}-point. p = 519521: the common roots are r = 37492 and 384583; both are x-coordinates of 𝔽\_p-points Q, but neither is the x-coordinate of a point of ⟨P̄⟩ (P̄ has order 698; equivalently 698Q ≠ O for both). So no prime divides both F\_n and G\_n. □ (Brute-force check: gcd = 1 for 1 ≤ n ≤ 150.) *Consequences.* (a) The parts of F\_n and G\_n prime to 6 are coprime, so the root numbers of E\_{t\_n} and E\_{t′\_n} involve disjoint sets of primes at the bad places coming from the sextic. (b) From Appendix B and F.21, ε\_nε′\_n = (−1)^n for all n ≥ 2 (ε\_n = +1 iff n ≡ 0, 1, 5 (mod 6), ε′\_n = +1 iff 3 | n). Hence, assuming the exact root-number formula of F.24, W(E\_{t\_n})·W(E\_{t′\_n}) = (−1)^n·λ(|F\_nG\_n|)·s\_n·κ\_nκ′\_n with s\_n = −1 exactly when n ≡ 4, 5 (mod 9) (F.20): the joint sign of the pair is the Liouville sign of the degree-12 value F\_nG\_n up to explicit periodic and squareful corrections. *What this does not give.* The multiplicativity λ(|F\_nG\_n|) = λ(|F\_n|)λ(|G\_n|) holds without coprimality; coprimality only excludes shared primes as a source of dependence and does not make the two Liouville signs independent. An algebraic relation forced by the involution x ↦ 2/x is excluded by F.19 (the ratio h(2/x)/h(x) is not in ℚ̄^×·ℚ̄(J)^{×2}, so no squareclass identity relates the two sequences). The joint parity λ(|F\_nG\_n|) is again a parity problem for a binary form (of degree 12) along the orbit, and the barrier of F.24 remains.

**F.26 The quotient sextic of the involution x ↦ 2/x (Proposition; verified by exact computation).** Let u = x + 2/x be the coordinate on the quotient J/⟨T⟩ = J′ (x′ = u − 2, φ(P) = (1, −1) on J′: Y² = X³ + 4X² − 4X) and R(U) = −54U⁶ + 648U⁵ − 1242U⁴ − 11404U³ + 62082U² − 113832U + 73224. *(a) Identities.* h(X)h\_T(X) = X⁶R(X + 2/X), and R(U) = Res\_Z(Z² − UZ + 2, h(Z)) is the quadratic norm of h; its roots are u\_i = x\_i + 2/x\_i for the six roots x\_i of h. *(b) Arithmetic of R.* R is irreducible over ℚ, as is R(X + 2) = −54X⁶ + 1998X⁴ − 4060X³ + 2730X² − 624X + 64; disc(R) = 2²⁰·3¹⁸·23²·519521² and disc(h) = 2⁶·3¹⁸·23² are both squares, with ratio 2¹⁴·519521²; R stays irreducible over ℚ(√d) for d ∈ {−1, ±2, ±3, ±5, ±6, ±7, ±23, ±69}; its Galois group has order 24 (the group S₄ of Appendix E, acting on the six roots inside A₆). Since R is irreducible of the same degree 6 as h, ℚ(u\_i) = ℚ(x\_i): R defines the same sextic field K = ℚ(α) as h (it is a Tschirnhaus transform, u = α + 2/α). *(c) The product as one value on J′.* For x(nP) = A\_n/B\_n², put p\_n = A\_n² + 2B\_n⁴ and q\_n = A\_nB\_n² (coprime). Then F\_nG\_n = R\_h(p\_n, q\_n), where R\_h is the binary sextic of R, and p\_n/q\_n − 2 is the x-coordinate of nφ(P) on J′ (checked for n ≤ 40): the product of the two sequences of F.25 is a degree-6 binary form along one orbit of the quotient curve. *(d) Where the exceptional primes of F.25 come from.* Res(h, X² − 2) = 2³·7·103: a root of h with x² = 2 is fixed by the involution and is automatically a common root of h and h\_T, which explains 2, 7, 103 (the common roots 4 mod 7 and 38 mod 103 are the square roots of 2). The prime 519521 is a genuine collision: h has the six roots 37492, 134940, 162299, 357224, 384583, 482031 modulo 519521 and two of them satisfy 37492·384583 ≡ 2, i.e. are swapped by the involution, so R has a double root and 519521² | disc(R)/disc(h). *(e) What it does not give.* The quotient does not lower the degree (it stays 6, irreducible, Galois group S₄), so the unconditional equidistribution results (irreducible factors of degree ≤ 3) remain inapplicable, F.19 excludes any squareclass identity between the two sequences, and the joint parity λ(|F\_nG\_n|) = λ(|R\_h(p\_n, q\_n)|) is again a parity problem for a sextic form along an orbit, now on J′. (Computations done in sympy, reproducing every assertion of the supplied Magma script.)

**F.27 Literature status: primitive divisors versus parity (Remark).** Two kinds of results must be kept apart. Primitive-divisor theorems say that new primes appear; Chowla-type theorems control the parity of the number of prime factors, which is what the root-number strategy needs. For the first kind, Verzobio (J. Number Theory 209 (2020) 378–390) proves that for {nP + Q} with Q a torsion point of prime order the denominator of x(nP + Q) has a primitive divisor for all large n, and relates the problem to the Lang–Trotter conjecture; his CM paper (arXiv:2010.10175) requires P non-torsion and endomorphisms f, g ≠ 0 with f(P) = g(Q), i.e. Q commensurable with P. A prime 𝔭 divides F\_n exactly when nP̄ ≡ ±R̄\_i (mod 𝔭) for a point R\_i with h(x(R\_i)) = 0; these points are non-torsion, defined over a field of degree 12 and not commensurable with P, so neither hypothesis holds, and his comparison of old and new primes (which uses rQ = O) does not carry over: old primes of F\_n lie in gcd(F\_n, B\_{n±k}) for k < n, and an unconditional gcd bound of Siegel/Vojta type would be needed (not checked to exist in this generality). Even a full primitive-divisor theorem for F\_n would give one prime not dividing any earlier F\_k; this is weaker in content than sqf(|F\_n|) → ∞ (F.18) combined with F.25 and the valuation laws, and gives no information on the parity of the number of the other primes. For the second kind, Helfgott proves Chowla for reducible and for irreducible cubic forms (arXiv:math/0408142 and its companion) and describes the parity problem as the obstruction beyond degree 3; these statements concern forms over pairs (x, y), not thin sequences. Two literature searches found no Chowla-type result along an elliptic divisibility orbit (this is a statement about those searches, not a proof that none exists). Conclusion: extending Verzobio's method to the divisor h(x) = 0 is a legitimate study of the prime structure of F\_n but is not a route to H.

**F.28 The exact root-number formula holds for every rational t (Theorem; proved; confirmed by an independent analytic computation; closes open problem 5).** For t = U/V in lowest terms put c₀ = U² + V², a = V² − U², b = 2UV (reduced to the primitive triple). *Theorem.* For every t, W(E\_t) = f(t)·∏\_{p ≥ 5, p | F(U,V)} (−(ab/p)), the product running over the distinct primes, with f(t) as in Appendix B; in particular the exponents of F need not be odd. *Proof.* On the model y² = x³ + a₄x + a₆, a₄ = 4(V² − U²)c₀³, a₆ = −16UVc₀⁵, one has the identity Δ = 2¹²c₀⁹F(U, V). Let p ≥ 5 divide F. If p | V² − U² then F ≡ −54V⁶ ≢ 0, and if p | c₀ then F ≡ −8V⁶ ≢ 0 (mod p); hence p ∤ c₀ and p ∤ c₄ = −48a₄. So the reduction at p is multiplicative of type I\_e with e = v\_p(F), whatever e is. The local root number of a multiplicative fibre is −1 if split and +1 if non-split, independently of e. It is split exactly when −c₆ is a square mod p, where −c₆ ≡ −6·UV·c₀ up to squares; since F ≡ 0, the proved square identity 3(α⁴ − 1) = s(α)² (F.8) gives (−3c₀(V² − U²)/p) = 1, hence (−c₆/p) = (ab/p) (checked in 618 cases with no exception). So w\_p = −(ab/p), and the primes not dividing F contribute through f(t). □ *Consequence.* The identity W = ε\_n·λ(F″\_n) holds exactly when every prime of F″\_n has odd exponent; in general W = ε\_n·δ\_n·κ\_n (F.24), and the assumption "exact root-number formula" made there is now a theorem. *Independent verification.* The sign was computed analytically from the functional equation (method validated on 11a, 14a, 389a with sign +1 and 37a with sign −1): for t = −4 (F = −3⁴·7², the case where the naive formula fails) the unique conductor consistent with the functional equation is 2⁵·3⁴·7·17² = 5243616 and the sign is +1, equal to the exact formula and different from the naive value −1; for t = 4 the sign is −1, as predicted. No other t with a square prime factor ≥ 5 and |F| ≤ 2·10⁶ exists.

**F.29 Status of the open items at the time of F.28 (superseded by §9 and F.46).** *Closed:* (1) the squareclass obstruction for the translate by T: h·h\_T is squarefree of degree 12 and coprime to x(x² − 2x + 2), so neither h·h\_T nor h·h\_T·x(x² − 2x + 2) is a square in ℚ̄(x), hence h·h\_T is not in ℚ̄^×·ℚ̄(J)^{×2} (this completes F.19 for the translation by T); (2) the exact root-number formula at squareful F\_n (F.28); (3) the period-3 law along nP + T (F.21); (4) the valuation laws at 2 and 3 (F.22); (5) the primitive-divisor route to H (F.27: not a route); (6) coprimality of the sequences along nP and nP + T (F.25). *Open but not used by any theorem:* the reducibility over ℚ̄ of the degree-5 invariant class (over ℚ it has no irreducible representative); the factorization of the 107-digit cofactor of F₁₀, which would add one data point to the table of λ(|F\_n|) and has no theoretical content. *Blocked:* Problem H itself. By F.24 it is equivalent (given the proved formulas) to the statement that W\_n = −1 does not hold for all large n, i.e. that the Liouville sign along the orbit does not eventually equal the explicit period-6 pattern −ε\_n on squarefree indices; every route examined in this paper (sieve methods, function-field algebra, the geometric multisection search F.13–F.15, Faltings F.18, primitive divisors F.27, the quotient F.26, coprimality F.25, local and global congruences (Appendix B, F.20, F.21)) leaves exactly this parity statement, which requires a Chowla-type input along a thin orbit.

### F.D Data, conjectures tested and refuted (F.30–F.42)

**F.30 Why C₂ and not C₄ or C₄′ (Remark; data computed exactly).** *Size of the members.* On C₂ (z = x(nP) on J, t\_n = 1 − z) the numerators and denominators of t\_n have 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 10, 12, 16, 19, 23, 27 digits for n = 1, …, 12; on C₄′ (v² = w³ + 8, R = (1, 3), t = (w − 2)/(w + 2)) they have 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 11, 15, 19, 24, 29, 35, 41 digits, i.e. the canonical height of the generator is about 1.5 times larger, and the members are correspondingly harder to compute with. *Number of rational points of height at most B* (all t = u/v with |u|, v ≤ 3000): C₂: 5, 5, 7, 7, 9, 9 for B = 10, 30, 100, 300, 1000, 3000; C₄′: 3, 5, 5, 6, 7, 7; the constant-x curve c = 4 (w² = (1 + t²)(3t² − t + 5)): 1, 2, 2, 2, 2, 2, namely only t = 4/3 and t = −11/2 (the two rank-2 fibres with W = +1), so its next members are astronomically large and no further data can be obtained from it; c = 1: 2, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4. *Structure.* Only C₂ is an elliptic curve in the form (z, w) with t = 1 − z a coordinate function whose denominators are squares (x-coordinates of rational points on J have square denominators), so V\_n = D\_n² and (2/c′) = 1; this is what makes Hilbert reciprocity eliminate every symbol except those at 2, 3, ∞ and allows the exact period-6 and period-3 theorems of Appendix B and F.21, the valuation laws of F.22, the coprimality of F.25 and the quotient description of F.26 (all of which use the rational 2-torsion point T = (0, 0) of J and the involution x ↦ 2/x). On C₄′ the denominator V is not a square, the factor (2/c) enters (observed period 2 along nR and period 1 along nR + T) and no corresponding theorem has been proved; for c = 4 there is no such parametrization at all. *What is the same.* All covers carry the same discriminant sextic D(t); the pulled-back divisor has irreducible factors of degree divisible by 6, the global formula reduces the sign question on C₄′ to the same parity statement as on C₂, and the unknown is the same bit δ\_n. So no cover is better for H itself; C₂ is the best for data and for proofs. (Not computed: the canonical height of the generator of the c = 4 Jacobian, for lack of PARI/Sage here; the point counts above are the proxy.)

**F.31 The one-bit test on the completely factored members (Observation; data recomputed from scratch).** For n = 2, …, 9 along nP and along nP + T the part N of |F| prime to 6 has been factored completely (the product of the primes was checked to equal N; every factor passed a strong probable-prime test, which has no known counterexample but is not a proof; all exponents are 1, so κ = 1). From each factorization I computed s = the number of split primes ((ab/p) = +1), f from the 2-adic and 3-adic tables, W = f·(−1)^s, ε = f·(ab/N) from the exact definition of Appendix B, and δ = (−1)^ω. Results: **W = ε·δ in 16 of 16 rows, and ε equals the proved law (period 6 along nP: +1 iff n ≡ 0, 1, 5 mod 6; period 3 along nP + T: +1 iff 3 | n) in 16 of 16 rows**, so the only data-dependent quantity is the single bit δ; for nP + T this also checks F.21 on members of size up to 71 digits. Table (kind, n, digits of N, ω, f, s, W, ε, δ): nP: (2, 5, 2, +, 1, −, −, +), (3, 10, 2, +, 1, −, −, +), (4, 18, 2, +, 1, −, −, +), (5, 29, 3, +, 1, −, +, −), (6, 41, 4, +, 2, +, +, +), (7, 55, 4, +, 2, +, +, +), (8, 73, 4, +, 1, −, −, +), (9, 91, 5, +, 0, +, −, −); nP + T: (2, 3, 1, −, 1, +, −, −), (3, 10, 3, −, 0, −, +, −), (4, 16, 2, +, 1, −, −, +), (5, 27, 2, −, 2, −, −, +), (6, 39, 3, +, 1, −, +, −), (7, 56, 5, +, 2, +, −, −), (8, 71, 4, +, 1, −, −, +), (9, 91, 4, −, 3, +, +, +). W = +1 occurs at n = 6, 7, 9 along nP and n = 2, 7, 9 along nP + T, i.e. at 6 of these 16 members. *A pattern, with a warning.* The bit δ equals +, +, +, −, +, +, +, − along nP and −, −, +, +, −, −, +, + along nP + T for n = 2, …, 9: both are exactly periodic of period 4 on these 8 terms. For independent fair signs the chance of a given period-4 pattern on 8 terms is 1/16 per sequence (1/256 for both), and about 8.6% per sequence (0.7% for both) that some period ≤ 4 fits, but this was noticed after looking at the data and the null model is doubtful: ω(N) grows roughly like 2 log n plus a constant (observed 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5 along nP), so for such small n the parity of ω changes at fairly regular steps, which is a transient effect and cannot persist, since the gaps between increments of ω grow with n. It is not a theorem and carries no weight for H; it is recorded because it is testable at once. *Pre-registered predictions at n = 10* (period 4 would give δ = + along nP and δ = − along nP + T). N(10P) = 547·2557·12323894684537·C₉₄ with C₉₄ = 2149588324331168952381234383582830746151739813353825687411724950397481307158392891625584263399 (94 digits, composite, no prime factor below 10⁶, not a perfect power): period 4 predicts that C₉₄ has an odd number of prime factors (three), whence ω even, δ = +, W(10P) = −1 (here ε₁₀ = −1); if C₉₄ is a product of two primes then δ = −1 and W(10P) = +1 (rank ≥ 2 under the parity conjecture). N(10P + T) = 1009·3580555487435623·22085981153898979·C₇₈ with C₇₈ = 477477696520876194622467752331032297023833278265538300772166527205974724500149 (78 digits, composite, no prime factor below 10⁶, not a perfect power): period 4 predicts that C₇₈ has an even number of prime factors (two), whence ω odd, δ = −, and since ε′₁₀ = −1, W(10P + T) = +1; if C₇₈ has three prime factors the pattern fails and W(10P + T) = −1. Neither cofactor could be factored with the tools available here (one core, no sieve software); a quadratic sieve or NFS implementation (Magma's Factorization, PARI's factor, msieve) should do the 78-digit number first.

**F.32 Result of the pre-registered test at 10P + T (Observation; factorization supplied by the user with PARI/GP, product and primality rechecked).** The 78-digit cofactor of F.31 factors as C₇₈ = 928615468896957507550954470402067379 · 514182363436225319787643322205506400496631 (36 and 42 digits, both probable primes, product equal to C₇₈). So N(10P + T) = 1009 · 3580555487435623 · 22085981153898979 · p₃₆ · p₄₂ has ω = 5, δ = −1 (as period 4 predicted), the Legendre symbols (ab/p) are +, +, +, −, + (s = 4), f = +1, hence W(E\_{t(10P+T)}) = f·(−1)^s = **+1**, equal to ε′₁₀·δ = (−1)(−1) as in F.31 (and ε′₁₀ = f·(ab/N) = −1 as F.21 requires). At t = 5623396687955417273/5979893522075890561 the point (2, y) lies on E\_t and is non-torsion (no multiple up to 12 is the point at infinity), so under the parity conjecture E\_{t(10P+T)} has rank ≥ 2 (or Sha is infinite). Including n = 10 the sign W = +1 now occurs at 7 of the 17 members known along nP (n ≥ 2) and nP + T (n ≥ 2). *How much this confirms.* The prediction that C₇₈ has an even number of prime factors was the a priori more likely outcome (a 78-digit composite with no prime factor below 10⁶ most likely has two prime factors), so the confirmation is weak evidence for a period-4 pattern; the sharper test is the 94-digit cofactor C₉₄ of F.31 along nP, for which period 4 predicts an odd number (three) of prime factors, the less likely outcome a priori.

**F.33 What a period-4 law for δ would imply (Remark; hypothetical; the pattern is refuted in F.38).** Suppose δ\_n = λ(|F\_n|) were +1 for n ≢ 1 (mod 4) and −1 for n ≡ 1 (mod 4) along nP, and δ′\_n = −1 for n ≡ 2, 3 and +1 for n ≡ 0, 1 (mod 4) along nP + T, as observed for 2 ≤ n ≤ 9 and (nP + T) n = 10. Together with the proved periodic laws for ε\_n and ε′\_n this would give W = ε·δ·κ with κ = 1 on squarefree indices and hence a period-12 sign: along nP, W = +1 exactly for n ≡ 0, 6, 7, 9, 11 (mod 12) (density 5/12); along nP + T, W = +1 exactly for n ≡ 0, 2, 7, 9, 10, 11 (mod 12) (density 1/2). These patterns reproduce all 17 signs computed so far. Then H would hold along both orbits, with positive density, and under the parity conjecture infinitely many members would have rank ≥ 2. The law differs from the H-false pattern δ = −ε exactly at the indices where W = +1 (n = 6, 7, 9, 11, 12, … along nP). The first untested predictions are W(11P) = W(12P) = W(11P + T) = W(12P + T) = +1, but these members have N of about 135 to 160 digits and cannot be factored; the only feasible test left is the 94-digit cofactor of F.31 at n = 10. *Why this is not a route to a proof.* The law would say that the Liouville sign along the orbit is a deterministic function of n mod 4, which contradicts the Chowla-type heuristic that predicts independent signs; no mechanism is known (a prime p divides F\_n for n in at most 12 residue classes ±k modulo the order of P̄ modulo a prime above p, but the parity of the number of such primes up to size e^{cn²} is not controlled by this, and squareclass identities of three kinds are proved impossible (F.19, F.28, F.34) while identities of other kinds have not been examined (F.34)). Since the size of N grows like e^{n²}, the law can never be tested for large n by factorization, so it would remain a conjecture even if every feasible check agreed.

**F.34 What is and is not excluded among algebraic routes, and the elliptic-net relation (Proposition; verified by computation).** *Correction.* The statement in F.33 that the algebraic routes are all excluded was too strong. *Proved excluded:* (i) squareclass relations between h(x(Q)) and h(x(\[m\]Q)) for |m| ≥ 2 and between h(x(Q)) and h(x(Q + T)) for the 2-torsion points T (F.19, F.28), as identities in ℚ̄(J); (ii) F\_n a square for infinitely many n (F.18); (iii) degree reduction by the quotient J → J′ (F.26) and by rational base change (barrier of §4); (iv) shared primes between the two sequences outside an explicit finite set (F.25). *New (the three-term relation).* For P, Q ∈ J with u = x(P), v = x(Q) and α a root of h one has the exact identity (u − v)²(x(P + Q) − α)(x(P − Q) − α) = N\_α(u, v) := (uv − 2)² − 2α\[(u + v)(uv + 2) − 4uv\] + α²(u − v)² (checked on exact points), so F(P + Q)F(P − Q) = Norm\_{K/ℚ}(N\_α(u, v))/(u − v)¹². The polynomial N\_α is irreducible over ℚ(α) as a polynomial in (u, v, α), squarefree in u, and coprime to u − α, v − α, ρ(u), ρ(v) and u − v (ρ(x) = x(x² − 2x + 2)); at u = α it equals −4ρ(α)v + (α² − 2)², a nonzero polynomial for every root α of h. Since the square classes in ℚ̄(J × J) of elements of ℚ̄(u, v) are those modulo squares and the factors ρ(u), ρ(v), no product of the form N\_α·(u − α)^i(v − α)^j·ρ(u)^kρ(v)^l with exponents in {0, 1} is a square. Hence there is no squareclass identity relating F(P + Q)F(P − Q) to F(P) and F(Q); the elliptic-net relation does not give a parity relation among the F\_n. *Bounded searches, not proofs:* second sections of twists (low height and degree), smooth rational curves of degree ≤ 4 and invariant classes of degree ≤ 21 (F.13–F.15), polynomial twists with coefficients ≤ 4 (§8). *Not examined:* arithmetic identities that hold only at the points nP and not as identities of functions (for example relations in the class group or unit group of the degree-6 field K or of the degree-12 field L between the ideals (A\_n − αB\_n²)); descent or Heegner-type constructions of a second point on E\_{t\_n} not coming from the parity theorem; and relations involving three or more terms F\_{n₁}, F\_{n₂}, F\_{n₃} beyond the three-term relation above. For ideals of K one fact is clear: every prime ideal dividing A\_n − αB\_n² (outside the primes dividing the index and the discriminant) has residue degree 1, because α reduces into 𝔽\_p, so Ω(F\_n) counts only degree-1 primes, and the evenness of the Frobenius classes (disc h is a square, Gal ⊂ A₆) constrains all primes above p together, not these alone.

**F.35 Conjecture D: δ\_{2n} = δ\_n (Hypothetical; refuted in F.38).** *Statement.* For all n ≥ 1, λ(|F\_{2n}|) = λ(|F\_n|) along the orbit nP. *Data.* With δ₁ = +1 (F₁ = −1) and δ₂, δ₄, δ₆, δ₈ = +, +, +, + the four testable cases δ₂ = δ₁, δ₄ = δ₂, δ₆ = δ₃, δ₈ = δ₄ hold (probability 1/16 for independent signs). The analogue along nP + T fails (δ′₄ = + ≠ δ′₂ = −, and δ′₁₀ = − ≠ δ′₅ = +), so D, if true, is not a symmetric property of the two orbits. *Consequence for H.* From Appendix B, ε\_{2n}ε\_n = (−1)ⁿ for all n (checked for n < 200 and clear from the table of ε modulo 6), hence W\_nW\_{2n} = (−1)ⁿ·δ\_nδ\_{2n}·κ\_nκ\_{2n}. Under D this is W\_nW\_{2n} = (−1)ⁿκ\_nκ\_{2n}: for odd n with κ\_nκ\_{2n} = +1 (for instance when F\_n and F\_{2n} are squarefree) exactly one of the members n, 2n has W = +1. So D would imply H as soon as infinitely many odd n have κ\_nκ\_{2n} = +1; this squarefreeness-type condition is not known to hold for infinitely many n. *Rival law and the experiment.* The period-4 law of F.33 violates D at n = 5 (it predicts δ₁₀ = +, D predicts δ₁₀ = δ₅ = −). The 94-digit cofactor C₉₄ of F.31 decides between them: a product of two primes gives ω(N(10P)) = 5, δ₁₀ = −1 (D holds, period 4 fails, W(10P) = +1); a product of three primes gives δ₁₀ = +1 (period 4 holds, D fails, W(10P) = −1). Two primes is the a priori more likely outcome, so a confirmation of D would again be weak evidence. *Why no algebraic mechanism exists.* By the doubling formula F\_{2n} = Res\_α(h, q\_α(A\_n, B\_n²)) = Norm\_{K/ℚ}(q\_α(A\_n, B\_n²)) with q\_α(a, b) = (a² − 2b²)² − 4αab(a² − 2ab + 2b²) (checked exactly for n ≤ 4, with ratio 1). D would follow at once from a squareclass identity φ(\[2\]Q)·φ(Q) ∈ ℚ̄^×·ℚ̄(J)^{×2}, which makes F\_{2n}F\_n a square up to a bounded factor; F.19 proves that this identity is impossible. In the cases computed F\_n and F\_{2n} are coprime, so D would be a deterministic correlation between the Liouville signs of two coprime numbers, in contradiction with the Chowla heuristic exactly as the period-4 law is.

**F.36 Independence of h and its doubling pullback modulo squares (Proposition; proved by exact computation; elementary form of the case m = 2 of F.19).** Let δ(X) = (X² − 2)²/(4X(X² − 2X + 2)) = x(2Q) as a function of X = x(Q), and Num(X) = Res\_α(h(α), (X² − 2)² − 4αX(X² − 2X + 2)), so that h(δ(X)) = Num(X)/(4X(X² − 2X + 2))⁶ (checked exactly). *Proposition.* The classes of h and of h(δ(X)) in ℚ(X)^×/ℚ^×·(ℚ(X)^×)² are independent: none of h, h(δ(X)), h·h(δ(X)) is a constant times a square in ℚ(X). *Proof.* The denominator (4X(X² − 2X + 2))⁶ is a square, so h(δ(X)) ≡ Num(X). Here Num has degree 24 and is irreducible over ℚ (computed), h is irreducible of degree 6, and gcd(h, Num) = 1 (computed). Then h, Num and h·Num each have an irreducible factor of multiplicity one, hence are not constants times squares. □ *Remarks.* (i) The facts used are the irreducibility of Num (degree 24) and gcd(h, Num) = 1, not the irreducibility and coprimality results for h, h\_T and R (F.25, F.26), which concern the involution x ↦ 2/x and not doubling; those facts were established only in the computation above. (ii) Over ℚ̄ the same holds: h·Num is squarefree of degree 30, and coprime to ρ(X) = X(X² − 2X + 2) (Num(0) = 4⁶, Num(1 ± i) ≠ 0), so h·Num·ρ^k is not a square in ℚ̄(X) either, which is the case m = 2 of F.19 in ℚ̄(J). (iii) The proposition excludes only identities of rational functions (F.34): it shows that Conjecture D (F.35) cannot be obtained from F\_{2n}F\_n = c·(square), and does not exclude arithmetic coincidences at the points nP.

**F.37 Conjecture D on the cover C₄′, and why a proof of D is not to be expected (Observation).** On C₄′ (v² = w³ + 8, R = (1, 3), t(nR) = (w(nR) − 2)/(w(nR) + 2)) the part N of |F(U, V)| prime to 6 was factored completely for n = 1, …, 7 (products checked, all factors probable primes); the number of prime factors counted with multiplicity Ω and δ = (−1)^Ω are: n = 1: N = 1471, Ω = 1, δ = −; n = 2: 859·5743, Ω = 2, δ = +; n = 3: 7²·19·173·233·3923578273 (18 digits, squareful), Ω = 6, δ = +; n = 4: 103·p₂₇, Ω = 2, δ = +; n = 5: 647264489·p₃₆, Ω = 2, δ = +; n = 6: eight distinct primes, Ω = 8, δ = +; n = 7: a prime of 18 digits times a prime of 69 digits, Ω = 2, δ = + (n = 8 has 110 digits and was not factored). The analogue of Conjecture D (δ\_{2n} = δ\_n) therefore fails at n = 1 (δ₂ = + ≠ δ₁ = −) and holds at n = 2 and n = 3. Counting all three orbits, the analogue of D holds in 8 of the 11 tests available (4 of 4 along nP on C₂, 2 of 4 along nP + T, 2 of 3 on C₄′), consistent with chance. *Why a proof is out of reach.* (i) By F.36, D cannot come from an identity of rational functions. (ii) F\_n and F\_{2n} have no common prime in the cases computed (more generally p | F\_n and p | F\_{2n} force nP̄ ≡ ±R̄ and 2nP̄ ≡ ±R̄′ modulo p, which restricts p to a thin set), so D would assert that the Liouville signs of two essentially disjoint sets of primes are equal for every n. (iii) Even a statement at the level of ideals of K would not suffice: every prime ideal dividing A\_n − αB\_n² has residue degree 1 (F.34), so the sign is (−1)^{number of prime ideals counted with multiplicity}, and an identity forcing that number to agree for two coprime ideals would have to hold with no square-class mechanism to explain it. (iv) If the signs behave like independent fair coins, as the Chowla heuristic predicts, D fails for infinitely many n with probability one; a proof of D would disprove that heuristic along the orbit. The sensible use of D is therefore as a test, not as a target: it predicts δ₁₀ = δ₅ = −1, equivalently that C₉₄ (F.31) is a product of two primes, which is the likely outcome a priori and therefore weak evidence if it occurs.

**F.38 The period-4 law and Conjecture D are refuted at n = 11 and n = 12 (Observation; complete factorizations, all factors probable primes, products checked).** *Along nP + T, n = 11* (135 digits): N = 491 · 37157059 · 181611589 · 3389375653 · 17257011513996899591 · 3508570895328349628798327471834055168176634467861009858298270589846759855273072797046367, six distinct primes, so ω = 6 and δ′₁₁ = **+1**; the Legendre symbols give s = 3, f = +1, W = −1, ε′₁₁ = −1 (as F.21 requires, 3 ∤ 11) and W = ε′δ′. The period-4 law of F.31 predicted δ′₁₁ = −1: **refuted.** *Along nP, n = 12* (163 digits): N = 23 · 83 · 18397 · 7851197 · 172604961553 · 3022285517285783321 · 63444875642234219830688037601870240082732857828883494817671516344727361247789078750329402859044190800003740275757566803, seven distinct primes, so ω = 7 and δ₁₂ = **−1**; s = 3, f = +1, W = −1, ε₁₂ = +1 (as F.17 requires, 12 ≡ 0 mod 6) and W = εδ. The period-4 law predicted δ₁₂ = +1, and Conjecture D (F.35) predicted δ₁₂ = δ₆ = +1: **both refuted.** These are also two further checks of the proved laws for ε (Appendix B at n = 12, F.21 at n = 11) on numbers of 163 and 135 digits, and of W = ε·δ. With these data W = +1 occurs at 7 of the 19 members known (nP and nP + T, n ≥ 2). Not factored: N(11P), a 136-digit composite with no prime factor below 2·10⁶ and none found by ECM with moderate bounds, 4013443238136047027943207311329615913049700315566516636507517490310778274740116167953214179614151497454294097047330487257135673478856081; N(10P) cofactor C₉₄ (F.31); N(12P + T) (a computation was still running). *Conclusion.* The period-4 pattern of δ was the transient effect anticipated in F.31: ω(N) grows roughly like 2 log n, so the parity of ω changes at regular steps for small n and then drifts. The data are again compatible with independent signs, and no pattern of δ is supported.

**F.39 Status of N(10P) and what the root-number formula gives from a partial factorization (Remark).** An ECM attempt on the 94-digit cofactor C₉₄ of F.31 (40 curves with B1 = 5·10⁴, 60 with B1 = 10⁵, 100 with B1 = 2.5·10⁵, sympy implementation, about 10 minutes) found no factor, which makes a prime factor of at most about 25 to 30 digits unlikely but proves nothing; the quadratic sieve or the number field sieve is needed, and the factorization is not yet known. *What is already determined.* For a squarefree N with a known part and a composite cofactor C, the exact formula W = f·(−1)^s gives (−1)^{s\_C} = (−1)^{ω(C)}·(ab/C) for the number s\_C of split primes of C, where (ab/C) is the Jacobi symbol; hence W = ε·δ, and the only missing information is the parity of ω(C). For N(10P): ε₁₀ = −1, ω(known part) = 3, so if C₉₄ is a product of two primes then ω = 5, δ₁₀ = −1 and W(E\_{t(10P)}) = +1; if it is a product of three primes then ω = 6, δ₁₀ = +1 and W = −1. In the first case Conjecture D (F.35) holds at n = 5 (the period-4 law is already refuted by F.38) and, with the non-torsion point (2, y) on E\_t, F.33 gives an unconditional member with rank ≥ 2 or Sha\[2^∞\] infinite. *Use of the formula.* It reduces the computation of W(E\_t) to the factorization of F(U, V) (no conductor or L-function computation) and was confirmed on members up to 163 digits (F.31, F.38) and by an independent analytic computation (F.28); it does not make the factorization cheaper, and it does not help to prove H.

**F.40 Problem H in terms of the root-number sums T\_r(X) (Reformulation; with independent verification of F.28).** *Exact formula.* Let N\_n be the part of |F\_n| prime to 6 (N\_n = |F\_n| along nP), ω the number of distinct prime factors, e\_p the exponent of p in N\_n, and η\_n = ∏\_{p | N\_n, e\_p even} (ab/p). Then for n ≥ 2, W\_n = ε\_{n mod 6}·(−1)^{ω(N\_n)}·η\_n (the same as W = ε·δ·κ of F.24, because (−1)^{ω}η = δκ with δ = (−1)^{Ω} and κ = ∏\_{e\_p even}(−(ab/p))); along nP + T replace ε\_{n mod 6} by ε′\_n (period 3). For squarefree N\_n, η\_n = 1, and the 19 factored members of F.31 and F.38 have all exponents equal to 1 (the product of the listed distinct primes equals N). *Sums.* For r mod 6 put M\_r(X) = #{n ≤ X : n ≡ r (mod 6)}, S\_r(X) = Σ (−1)^{ω(N\_n)} and T\_r(X) = Σ W\_n over the same indices; then T\_r = ε\_r·Σ (−1)^{ω(N\_n)}η\_n, equal to ε\_r·S\_r on squarefree indices, and #{n ≤ X, n ≡ r, W\_n = +1} = (M\_r(X) + T\_r(X))/2. Problem H is equivalent to sup\_X Σ\_r (M\_r(X) + T\_r(X)) = ∞ (a restatement). *Sufficient conditions.* |T\_r(X)| ≤ (1 − c)M\_r(X) for all large X and some c > 0 and one residue class r (both signs of W with positive lower density) implies H; so does limsup (M\_r + T\_r) = ∞ for one r, which is H restricted to a class. Full cancellation T\_r = o(X) is not needed. The relevant auxiliary quantity is a\_n = (−1)^{ω(N\_n)}η\_n, not the unweighted parity S\_r: bounding S\_r says nothing about H unless η is controlled, and the pattern that must be excluded is a\_n = −ε\_n for all large n. *The squareful correction.* The set of n with F\_n not squarefree has positive lower density: for each prime p of good reduction the set {n : p² | F\_n} is periodic with period p·r\_p, so it is a nonempty union of residue classes as soon as one n is known (p = 23, n = 32), hence of positive density.
